====================================================================================================

Every Windows 11 app should kill Electron, and Discord just showed why
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-07
11:11:15 UTC
20:11:15 KST

Discord’s new Rust client had its friends list loaded before the current Electron app could get past its loading screen, in a 15-second clip that we found on the X account of Discord co-founder and CTO Stanislav Vishnevskiy. The video had 2.3 million views at the time of writing, and Discord employees have confirmed that both apps were launched from a cold start.
Vishnevskiy also revealed that the 290.2MB RAM usage on the Task Manager of the new client, which shows up as Discord Phoenix, came with two accounts signed in at once, which justifies the already efficient RAM usage.
Discord’s Rust client opens before the Electron app finishes loading
“The new Discord client using less RAM is just part of the story. Clicking around feels instant, I can’t go back. Check out the boot time,” Vishnevskiy wrote on X, and Discord’s official account also shared the clip with the current client on the left and the Rust rebuild on the right.
We went through the video frame by frame. What’s impressive is that the current client is opened first and shows a blank dark window for about three seconds, followed by a tip that reads “In the ancient days, Discord started as a browser-only app.” Funnily enough, the Electron app still carries a browser engine today.
The new client is double-clicked roughly a second and a half later, yet its window fades in within a second. When the old app reaches its interface, about five seconds after launch, the Rust version already has the friends list loaded, while the Electron client briefly says “There are no friends online at this time.”
Of course, people on X asked if the new app simply popped out of the system tray. “Both are cold starts, it’s really that fast. We’ve been using it internally now for a while and the first time I tried it I thought I’d forgot to close it when I opened it fresh,” replied Coral, who leads audio and video at Discord.
Travis, who works on socials and community at Discord and filmed the clip, added that a tray launch would mean “the friends list would have already been loaded!” Vishnevskiy thanked him for “finding these janky old laptops,” which are ThinkPads with a Copilot key, so 2024 or newer, which I wouldn’t dare call janky or old. They’re beautiful machines!
The new Discord app skips the “Starting” screen and updates in the background
As you’d already know, the current app usually checks for updates and shows a “Starting” screen when you open it, which one person on X said “takes like 5-10s every time.” Asked why the new client skips it, coral said, “It bootstraps so fast + is a new architecture so we don’t need that modal at all. We auto-update in the background.”
Discord isn’t handing updates to the Microsoft Store or Windows’ Update Orchestration Platform either, as Coral said “We do not at this moment” when asked.
Discord’s Rust client uses about 70% less RAM with two accounts signed in
When one reply called 290MB “still vile” for a chat app, Vishnevskiy said, “Those 4k avatar decorations gotta render somewhere ;) but joking aside this client is actually running 2 accounts concurrently which is supported in new client.”
Today’s Account Switcher lets you add up to five accounts and swap between them. Asked if the new client could keep work and personal accounts running “without needing to tear down the client in between,” Vishnevskiy replied “Yup.” Again, impressive and exciting.
For comparison, a Discord employee said in December 2025 that normal usage is under 1GB, while the company tested auto-restarting the app at 4GB. So, 290.2MB is about 70% below the 1GB mark, and on our PCs, the Electron app usually idles at 1-2GB. To be clear, it’s a snapshot from an early build, and Discord hasn’t published a like-for-like benchmark of both clients, but we’ll be doing that test when we get hands-on with the Rust version.
Discord did try to fix Electron from the inside. Its September patch notes say the Electron 42 upgrade cut median CPU usage by about 17%, but “at the cost of a smaller regression to p50 memory usage (~4%).” Even the Game Mode toggle it tested later doesn’t mention RAM.
Discord is replacing Electron with GPUI, not Tauri
Electron inherits its multi-process architecture from Chromium, so every Electron app comes with its own browser engine, with a main process and a renderer process for each window. Tauri, which many guessed Discord would pick, uses Rust for app logic but builds the interface in HTML, CSS and JavaScript inside the system’s webview, which is WebView2 on Windows.
Discord went with GPUI, “A fast, hybrid immediate and retained mode, GPU-accelerated UI framework for Rust” from the makers of the Zed code editor. GPUI draws the interface on the GPU without any HTML, and Rust, which Discord has used since 2018, manages memory without a garbage collector.
Why not WinUI, you ask? (At least, I thought of it). Discord hasn’t mentioned it, and it was unlikely to, as Microsoft’s native framework only runs on Windows, while GPUI already powers Zed on Windows, macOS and Linux. Discord has its own patches on top of GPUI, and Vishnevskiy said “we will see what we can upstream if relevant to the community.”
Web tech stays where it makes sense, as YouTube embeds and Activities will use the system webview. “We use the native webview similar to Tauri,” Vishnevskiy said about Activities.
Discord’s mobile apps drop React Native, and the web app moves to WebAssembly
Phones keep native interfaces but will share app code with desktop through Rust. On React Native, Vishnevskiy explained, “Since the beginning the iOS app started as React native, Android was ported to React Native couple years ago to achieve parity as it was always lagging in features.”
The browser version will keep rendering HTML, but “logic will be WASM instead of JS.” WebAssembly (WASM) lets code written in languages like Rust run inside a browser, so the web app can share the Rust code too. As for the look, whether you like it or not, “The app looks and works identically to the current one,” Vishnevskiy said.
Discord’s Rust client goes into private testing soon
If you can’t to try it out, Discord marketing director Ryan K. Rigney said, “Going into private testing soon. Gonna take our time to make sure it’s stable and secure first. Won’t be long, tho.” I would rather wait for them to iron out any bugs.
Mark Smith, Discord’s VP of Engineering, added that “accessibility and security are just as important to us as performance,” and Coral confirmed the new client keeps your login token in the system keyring, which is Credential Manager on Windows 10 and 11.
Of course, Discord is not just doing all this work for Windows. When asked if it works on Linux, Rigney replied, “Yes,” and Coral said, “a lot of people run Mac internally!” Discord hasn’t announced a public release date yet.
Discord just made WhatsApp and Teams look worse on Windows 11
Electron made it easier for Discord to ship one desktop app on Windows, macOS, and Linux for a decade. But Discord has now shown that a chat app with millions of users can drop the bundled browser without a redesign.
As for the owners of the biggest messaging platforms for regular users and enterprises, Meta and Microsoft are running out of excuses. WhatsApp went from a native app that idled under 100MB to a WebView2 wrapper using up to 1.2GB, and I uninstalled it. Microsoft Teams also uses WebView2, took up 963MB doing nothing in our tests, and it doesn’t look like Redmond cares enough to go native, despite pushing WinUI.
Ironically, AI got us here. Data centers soaked up DRAM supply, prices shot up, and Windows chief Pavan Davuluri now says “memory is top of mind for us right now in the industry” as Microsoft trims Windows 11’s RAM usage. Apps that kept asking for more RAM are finally going on a diet, and we’ve come full circle.
Are you waiting to try the Rust-based Discord when it rolls out?

====================================================================================================

Cryosphere Faces Permanent Damage When World Overshoots 1.5C, Scientists Warn
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-07
11:00:00 UTC
20:00:00 KST

Cryosphere Faces Permanent Damage When World Overshoots 1.5C, Scientists Warn 55
			
		 	
				A UN-backed report warns that even temporarily overshooting the Paris agreement's 1.5C warming target could cause permanent and widespread losses of glaciers, snow and polar ice sheets. With Greenland and Antarctic ice sheets already melting about six times faster than in the 1990s, the report argues that global temperatures should ultimately be brought back below 1C. The Guardian reports:  The report, which comes as world leaders and diplomats converge on Turkey for the 31st annual Cop climate conference, documented rapid and accelerating melting of the cryosphere and polar oceans, with damages that range from the loss of the Greenland and Antarctic ice sheets, driving sea level rises that threaten hundreds of millions of people, to thawing permafrost, which releases more planet-heating gas. Glaciers, which are still responding to historical warming, would only preserve about 70% of their current volume over a timescale of centuries if long-term heating were brought back down to 0.8C, the report found.
Meanwhile, the ice sheets covering Greenland and Antarctica, which are melting about six times faster today than in the 1990s, would continue to lose ice even in scenarios where overshoot is brief and temperature rise returns to 1.5C, the report found. The scientists said a safe, long-term temperature limit for polar ice sheets lies much closer to 1.0C of global heating, and possibly even lower. "Snow and ice respond rapidly to every fraction of a degree of warming," said [James Kirkham, a glaciologist at ICCI and the lead author of the report. "But most of these systems will take centuries or millennia to recover after overshoot, especially if tipping points are crossed that cause changes to become essentially irreversible."
Last month, a UNEP report found the world must cut emissions by 18% by 2030 and by 45% by 2035 to have a good chance of keeping temperatures from rising 1.7C above preindustrial levels. It found there were "no good outcomes" of remaining above 1.5C. The cryosphere report found that current policies, which will result in 2.8C of global heating, would reduce the mass of glacier ice to below 10% in the Rockies, Alps, Iceland and Scandinavia. If warming was limited to 1.5C, about 20-35% of the mass would remain. In the Hindu Kush Himalayas, where glaciers are a source of water for nearly a quarter of humanity, current policies will leave just 41% of the ice mass. If warming was limited to 1.5C, 83% would remain.

Every Mac comes with an (undocumented) family member classifier
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-07
09:46:03 UTC
18:46:03 KST

What if I told you that every Mac since 2022 ships a pair of daemons whose only purpose in life is to build a graph about you and the people around you: who you are, who you know, how those people are related to you and to each other, how you communicate with them, where you go, with whom, what you do and when. This graph is built on device by cross-referencing pretty much everything on the machine: Contacts, Calendar, Photos, Messages, Mail, location, Screen Time and a long tail of behavioral signals. And, more importantly, it is held as a queryable store that LLMs retrieve from (allegedly). Would you believe me?
Well, that's what is happening, and interestingly enough, there is little documentation of how this whole mechanism works on your Mac. The closest thing to an official description is the man page (man 8 knowledgeconstructiond), and this is all you get:
Previous work: To be fair, Apple's own researchers did publish a paper on the techniques behind this graph construction, back in 2023. But there is next to nothing on how it is integrated into macOS: which versions run it, and the exact list of what is collected and inferred. And not a word about the "kinship" inference discussed below. Also, we personally ran into it while doing some security research on the Apple Intelligence bundle in macOS 26 in August 2026; but a few months before, Nic Lydon came across the same database and wrote it up in I Built a Personal Knowledge Graph. Apple Had Already Built One on My Laptop. And even more recently, a forensics tool went looking on the phone: in September 2026, Alexis Brignoni taught iLEAPP to read the same store on iOS, where graph.db and the ontology.db that decodes its predicate codes sit under /private/var/mobile/Library/IntelligencePlatform; he writes it up in Reading the iPhone Knowledge Graph, and Checking Its Work.
But we go a bit deeper.
This blog post is an account of some analysis work we carried out on what the knowledge graph actually does, so that you (the user) can get a better understanding of how this mechanism works, and some clarity on what the graph holds about you and your family and friends. Everything in it was collected on macOS 26 (26.6.2 at the time of writing).
To this end, we open-source a local dashboard (iKnow) you can use to read the content of your personal knowledge graph on your Mac.
Finally, we derail a bit and look into what we do best: weaponizing and exploiting. We run a thought experiment (one that applies to any such personalized system) and show what happens if such a knowledge base ends up in the wrong hands, and how a "privacy single point of failure" can be exploited to threaten you in ways you may not have considered before (when combined with an abliterated LLM).
What's in the personal knowledge graph?
The two daemons keep their work in ~/Library/IntelligencePlatform, a directory of SQLite databases (fifty-one databases) stored in plaintext but located in a zone protected by TCC Full Disk Access.
Likely, the most interesting is graph.db. It is a triple store written in a vocabulary of 181 entity classes and 278 predicates: every row is a subject, a predicate and an object. The subject is an entity (a person, an app, an event); the object is either a plain value (a name, a handle, a date) or another entity, which is where the graph gets its edges. The predicate is the interesting part: it says what kind of relationship the system believes holds between the two.
Most of what the two daemons do is populate those predicates, and the tables kept beside them, from everything the OS will let them read. Into the graph go Contacts; Photos (faces); Calendar (titles and attendees); Family Sharing (including the age of every member); the Medical ID emergency contact in Health; events and contacts Siri Suggestions finds in Mail and Messages; people donated by apps through App Intents; and installed apps. Beside it, in tables of their own, sit who appears with whom in your photos; the communication log (who, when, which app, which direction; no content), which also pulls from your other devices; location and movement; and a long tail of device signals (screen locked, Wi-Fi, Bluetooth, charging, ambient light, and a stream named ContextualUnderstanding.PhysicalSocial whose record format lists people by name, phone and email, each with a start and an end time; it reads like a log of who was physically around you, though we never saw it filled in).
What is inferred
The complete list of the 181 entity classes:
Fifty-seven of the 181 describe an activity: sleeping, exercising, mindfulness, going to bed, commute, dining, most of them with a matching type class beside them, so nearly a third of the vocabulary went on what you are doing rather than on who or where you are. That said, it is not clear that every class is actually used; some may be placeholders that are declared and never filled. In the table, we flag as implemented the classes for which we could locate a mechanism that might be responsible for the class, but those are to be taken as inference work rather than ground truth.
And this is the complete list of predicates (same disclaimer applies):
How it is inferred
The methods run the whole range. Most of the vocabulary is filled by plain copying (a name from Contacts, an attendee from Calendar) and rules over the copies; the interesting rows come from models: a pair of XGBoost classifiers decides who is family from 144 mostly behavioral features, two embedding models are one of ten signals the system uses to decide when two records are the same person, twelve analyzers in Photos score partner, parent, child, coworker and friend from who appears in your pictures with you, on which trips and holidays, and Apple's on-device LLM reads your messages to put a name and a relationship on a face (if Apple Intelligence is active).
Whatever the method, every row lands in the graph with a confidence attached, a number between 0 and 1.
The family scores live apart. Two CoreML models ship next to the ontology, EntityTagging_Family.mlmodelc and EntityTagging_FamilyAndFriends.mlmodelc: binary XGBoost classifiers that take 144 features and return a class and a probability over it. Their scores do not go into the triple store but into a table of their own (entityTagging.db, one row per person, seventeen relationship columns from family to mother to coworker, recomputed every day), which is what the iKnow scorecard further down reads. Only two of those seventeen columns have a shipped model behind them; what fills the other fifteen, and how any of these probabilities would become a confidence in the graph, we could not trace.
The feature names are in each model's metadata.json, in plain text. They are the clearest statement of what this system is that I have found anywhere. All 144:
The identity group is the tidy story: same surname, same home address, an iCloud Family flag — and it is the small half. The bulk of the model is the other two: who you message and who appears in your photographs, conditioned on the day of the week and the time of day, and then on both at once. photosPersonPosteriorProbabilityGivenSaturdayNight is a shipped feature name in a shipped classifier whose job is deciding whether someone is family.
Deleting something does not delete it everywhere.
The live graph forgets. Delete a contact and nothing re-derives them; the next run replaces the table wholesale.
But the store keeps a second table, expired_stable_graph, beside the live one. The retracted row moves there and keeps its confidence and its sources. How long it stays there is not clear from what we could see. It seems to go after about 48 hours (the store keeps a prune watermark, and a recent snapshot of the table reached back no further than that); but on my own Mac, I could still read very old records in it.
And a conclusion can outlive the evidence for it. On my Mac, the scorecard still scored two people the graph had dropped the day before, both of them known to it only from a calendar invitation. Well, if you want your partner not to discover some contacts you might have deleted and their "closeness" to you, better to drop some rows from expired_stable_graph too (unfortunately, you have to do it manually). But I am sure you have nothing to hide (just joking).
Look into your own data
Is any of this sensitive? We'll leave that to you, since "sensitive" means different things to different people (though I think you have plenty to choose from here). However, the thing is, none of it lives in isolation. It is built to be joined and correlated; the nodes are there to be linked:
The most important point, though, is that this is not only about you (the person using the Mac). The people around you are in it too: your family, your friends, "anyone" you have talked to through your device. None of them was asked, and the store links them to you and, through the events and groups they share, to each other. A relationship is intelligence one level above the rows it was inferred from. For instance, by correlating across my own store, I (well, an LLM working for me) could easily work out the following:
- That there is a child in the household, and roughly how old. The family-member types and a family circle give the household. The scorecard's child and son/daughter columns give the child's place in it. Beside it sits an age, copied from Family Sharing; and dates of birth in the store can carry the system's own word imputed, though what the system means by it is not clear to us.
- Which daycare the child goes to, and both of the numbers that reach it. Copied off contact cards. Note what is not there: the calendar has no school-run event, so the store holds the institution and its lines but not the hour the child is dropped off. That is still enough to call a parent from a number their phone already trusts.
- Where the family will be, on which days, between which hours. One calendar event, a place, two stored time slots and a title that names the family (so the guest list is in the string even where the attendee rows are empty).
- Which of the family will be there. The scorecard names the ones scored as family, a household entity holds them together, and a Family Sharing circle says which of them are in the same family group.
- And, in the same file, what is installed on the machine, down to the password manager.
It is easy to see how this could be weaponized if this single point of failure for your privacy ends up in the wrong hands. We play that (hypothetical) scenario out in a second write-up. The good thing is that this knowledge is meant to stay local on your machine, protected by the Full Disk Access permission; although it can be consumed by other services (but it is not entirely clear to us how and what can be accessed).
The reality is that the amount of information in this store is really something else: every time we dig into what the daemons infer, and how, a few new lines of investigation open up. Whatever you would rather no one knew about you or your family may well be in there somewhere, and we think you have the right to know whether it is. What you get instead: no documentation, no way to inspect the store and no general way to correct an inference that is wrong.
So we give you two tools. The first is iKnow, a local dashboard for browsing your own graph without anything leaving your Mac (the next section walks through it). The second is for going deeper. The iKnow repository ships a manual for your own agent that tells it where the databases are and how to read them, so it can go looking for whatever you are after. (Please point a self-hosted model at it, not a cloud one.)
iKnow
We designed iKnow as a web dashboard implemented in the Python standard library; no npm and no CDN.
It is meant to be run directly on your Mac, where the databases are stored. When executed, it binds 127.0.0.1, locates the databases in your file system (granted the necessary permissions), opens them read-only, collects the info on the fly and represents it on a dashboard. Nothing is written and nothing is logged; values go from the stores, over loopback, into a browser tab; it can be run air-gapped if you want.
We have tested it on macOS 26.6.2 and 26.7.1, and, only partially, on 27.0.1.
iKnow has a dozen pages.
Start here
What your Mac knows. The landing page: the graph of people around you. Below it come the faces in your photos, your family, your handles, your correspondents and where you will be next.
Its graph is the same household as the one at the top of this post, this time drawn by the tool's own renderer:
The graph is the overview, and an overview is easy to shrug at. The pages behind it are not.
Social graph. Who the store links to whom through shared meetings, group chats and a closeness score. Each circle opens to its members and to the evidence behind it.
What it concluded
Kinship scorecard. The family bindings inferred by the daemon.
Its card on the landing page is cut down to the people it names:
The card is a summary of one table, and iKnow draws the whole table too:
The scores in these two shots are invented, and more generous than the real thing. On the Mac we took apart, only mother reached the top of the ladder; the few others it scored at all got one small vote on every family label at once, father and brother alike. What the fixture keeps is the table's grain: it only ever writes a handful of values (0.125, 0.25, 0.5 and 0.96), and most people sit on one default.
The face index. A face cluster for everyone in your photos.
Its co-occurrence graph does not use a single contact, message or calendar entry. It is built from your photographs alone:
This is the page's own honest point: the graph points at the face clusters and can name almost none of them (three of the twenty-nine, in the fixture). It does not need to. Who stands next to whom, how often, and which groups never meet is all there without a single name, and whoever knows the household can put the names back on in a second.
Guessed or given. What you typed in and what the Mac worked out for itself live in the same table. A confidence score is the only thing separating them.
What it recorded
A year of contact. Who you talked to, when, and not a word of message text (and it does not need any to tell when you are asleep).
Its card on the landing page is the one that gets the reaction, and it contains no inference at all.
iKnow draws that line too, from the same log folded onto a single week:
That is the general shape of this store: almost nothing in it had to be collected, because almost everything in it is a consequence of something that was.
The calendar. The one layer that knows where you will be: the hour, the room, the attendees and the dial-in.
What you withdrew. Withdrawing a fact does not remove it. The row moves to an expired table and keeps its confidence and provenance.
The machinery
The store. The file itself: what it models, and two real rows expanded into everything they touch.
Device fingerprint. Every app installed on the Mac is written down in plain text, next to a token that is an unsalted SHA-256 of that same app identifier, so the page recomputes it in your browser and you can watch it match.
Who else reads it. The graph is the first copy, not the only one. This page maps the views that subscribe to it, the databases they publish into, the tables that repeat the same facts, the streams coming in from hardware paired with the Mac, and the machinery that is set up but has not run yet.
A note on macOS 27. macOS 27 was released on September 14, 2026. After the upgrade to 27.0.1, the graph withdrew most of the rows it had built from Contacts, Calendar, Siri Suggestions, Family Sharing and the app catalog, and rebuilt them over the following day. Some pages of iKnow may not work fully on macOS 27 yet.
But let's go deeper:

Running a law firm on Free software
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-07
09:39:06 UTC
18:39:06 KST

Running a law firm on Free software (2026 edition)
I started running decoded.legal - a small English law firm, focussing on Internet, telecoms, and tech law - on Free software, both client and server side, around five years ago. I have used Free software in my personal life for a lot longer than that.
Back in 2022, I wrote about my experiences after the first year or so.
I wrote an update in 2024.
Someone asked me recently what I am doing now, and, while it is mostly the same, some bits have changed. So here is the 2026 update.
Client-side stuff
I am still using Debian, with GNOME, and it works well for me.
I’m using it on a ThinkPad X1 Yoga Gen 6, as my primary machine, and, being a “Yoga” version, I rather like being able to fold the screen back and write on it to mark up documents. (Here are my thoughts on the different “Active Pens” available for it.)
However, the machine itself seems to have a hardware problem with the Thunderbolt / USB-C ports (fine for power, very flaky for data), and so I might need to replace it. And I have no idea what laptop I might get.
- Email (MUA): Thunderbird and mutt, depending on what I am doing 
  - Quick Folder Move and Move Message And Yourself are particularly useful add-ons for Thunderbird, for rapid filing of email with keyboard shortcuts
  - Thunderbird seems to be having a problem with some recurring
calendar invitations, while ikhal is handling them fine.
I’ve not dug into it, but it is a nuisance.
- Document production: LibreOffice’s Writer if I
want a “Word” document (e.g. I am exchanging documents with other
lawyers), and I am using
pandoc and typst to convert
Markdown files that I tend to write in vim for some
other bits. I’d like to explore tex at some point, as I
have a client who likes diffs to their .tex files, and that looks rather
neat.
- Presentations: reveal.js 
  - I absolutely love this.
  - I have a bash script which sets up a template for a new presentation, and then I just write the presentation using Markdown. I get a nicely-formatted presentation (using a css template) with very little effort, and no distracting GUI
  - Blogpost about reveal.js
  - Here are some presentations I’ve given, using reveal.js
- File manager: Nautilus 
  - Mainly because it is GNOME’s default, although, especially when combined with some useful bash scripts, it works well for my needs
- PDFs: evince for most reading; Xournal++ for marking up PDFs; PDFarranger and pdftk for interfering with PDFs
- Text expansion: espanso 
  - This is a fantastic shortcut for me, saving me from typing the same thing multiple times. I have loads of shortcuts set up, and I’d be lost without them.
- Browser: Firefox or links 
  - I am still using Firefox. It is … fine? I should probably look at LibreWolf or another fork, as I’m not enamoured by Mozilla’s direction of travel but, for now, Firefox it is.
  - I use Firefox for joining people’s Teams and Zoom etc. meetings, if someone else sets up the meeting, and that works fine
- Local wiki / knowledge management: vimwiki 
  - because I use vim so much, vimwiki was quite an obvious
choice.
  - I use this for keeping track of my professional development, and for various notes
- Disk encryption: LUKS
- Client-side encryption of files before syncing them with Syncthing: Cryptomator
- Fediverse: I tend to use the Mastodon advanced web UI or, when I want fewer images, the TUI toot. 
  - I schedule toots - for example, about blogposts, using a simple bash script and a cron job; it has moved on a bit since I wrote that post, but it is the same principle
- Password manager: Bitwarden, using the
vaultwarden server, with YubiKeys for managing more
sensitive credentials
- Markdown editor (for blogposts; I do presentations in vim or gedit): Apostrophe
- Scanning: paperless-ngx
- Label printing: there’s a great FOSS web UI for Brother (and perhaps other) label printers. I don’t print many labels, as we aim to be paperless, but it’s convenient when I need to do so
Why so many terminal-based tools? The simple answer is that I am very
comfortable in a terminal, and I like them. They have fewer distractions
and, when compared with most GUI software, are just - IMHO - easier to
use, especially when I don’t need to move my hands away from the
keyboard. So, while I don’t use them exclusively, I use
mutt, vim, ikhal,
ncmpcpp, newsboat, toot,
links, and various other TUIs, frequently.
Server-side stuff
Everything here - other than Signal - is self-hosted, and I’m mostly using Intel NUCs now. I still have some Raspberry Pi devices, but fewer than I did.
(Again, this doesn’t list everything; just the main applications.)
- Email (MTA): postfix / dovecot 
  - I need to move our mail infrastructure at some point, and I want to look at Stalwart.
- File synchronisation: Syncthing 
  - One of the bigger changes since last time was moving away from Nextcloud. I just was not getting on with it, and it was causing me more problems than it solved. So I switched to Syncthing for file sync, and radicale for CalDAV / CardDAV. I’ve been more than happy with this combination.
- Video conferencing: Signal 
  - I ran jitsi for years, but stopped using it a few months back. Jitsi is great, and I remain a fan, but most of the time I am joining other people’s Teams or Zoom meetings, so I was running the Jitsi server for very little usage. I turned it off, and instead offer Signal. I have had no grumbles.
- Invoicing and tracking payments: InvoicePlane, just for generating PDF invoices. Clients don’t interact with it
- Time tracking: Kimai 
  - v2 of Kimai is excellent, and I had a very pleasant exchange with the developer, which is always nice.
- “Case management” / “Matter management”: EspoCRM
- VPN: WireGuard, using trailofbit’s algo ansible scripts on one machine, and PiVPN on another (albeit not on a Pi)
- Vulnerability scanning: greenbone/OpenVAS
- Monitoring: Uptime Kuma 
  - I implemented this not long after the last update. It … just works?
- Forge / hosted git repository: Forgejo 
  - I use this for version tracking the website, and for a couple of template projects
  - I do not host anything particularly important on it - for that, I have a git process running on another machine, which is not accessible over the Internet, and I just push to that
- SIEM: Wazuh
- Password manager: vaultwarden
- Website: it is a static site built via mkdocs, hosted on apache2 
  - It got even simpler and even faster to load. Yes, it’s basic, but I like it!
  - We still have an .onion version. If you visit decoded.legal in Tor Browser, it should re-direct your automatically
- Blog: BSSG 
  - After a couple of annoyances with hugo, I moved to BSSG - a bash static site generator. The developer, Stefano, is amazing, and it is working incredibly well. I’ve gone for a very basic, easy to read, theme - which should be very similar to what was there before - and there’s still an RSS feed for it, of course
  - I blog quite frequently, and, rather than keep private / internal “knowhow” files, I tend to just blog things and share the learnings
  - I tend to use my personal blog for posts about the more technical bits and pieces
  - I have automated the most common actions - templates, and publishing - using bash scripts, so that I can focus on writing (in Markdown); getting it online is the easiest bit
- Fediverse: I am using the *glitch-soc Mastodon** fork 
  - Switching from normal Mastodon to glitch-soc Mastodon was easy
  - I stopped using Markdown in toots, even though I like the rendered output (i.e. as html not as markdown), but this is not supported consistently well across clients, so I stopped using it.
  - I turned off decoded.legal’s own fedi instance. I had been on the fence as to whether this is worthwhile or not for a while, and decided that, really, it was not.
- Knowledge management, and managing our law firm’s (numerous) policies and procedures: dokuwiki with the Markdown plugin
- DNS: unbound, with Pi-Hole in front of it 
  - I used to run a doh server, but I’ve stopped doing that.
- Backups: restic and rsync
- Forms: LiberaForms 
  - I have been really happy with LiberaForms, for easy-to-create and easy-to-use forms, especially since they implemented encryption, so that the form responses are stored encrypted on the server. I still delete them as soon as I can, but knowing that they are encrypted in the database is welcome.
  - I use LiberaForms for our “Know Your Client” pre-engagement questionnaire, and for various other bits
- Collaborative document editing: I don’t. 
  - I used to run CryptPad, but it just didn’t get much use, so I turned it off.
AI tools
Mobile device
I’m using a Pixel running GrapheneOS for my mobile device.
I use F-Droid as the main app store.
Using Android doesn’t thrill me, especially given Google’s direction of travel, but at the moment - despite trying quite a lot of different OSs and devices, including postmarketOS and Ubuntu Touch, as well as SailfishOS previously - I have not found something that does what I need better than GrapheneOS.
Working with others (who don’t use FOSS tools)
No change here: zero problems.
Many of my clients use Microsoft’s software, or Apple’s software.

Running a law firm on Free software (2026 edition)
I started running decoded.legal - a small English law firm, focussing on Internet, telecoms, and tech law - on Free software, both client and server side, around five years ago. I have used Free software in my personal life for a lot longer than that.
Back in 2022, I wrote about my experiences after the first year or so.
I wrote an update in 2024.
Someone asked me recently what I am doing now, and, while it is mostly the same, some bits have changed. So here is the 2026 update.
Client-side stuff
I am still using Debian, with GNOME, and it works well for me.
I’m using it on a ThinkPad X1 Yoga Gen 6, as my primary machine, and, being a “Yoga” version, I rather like being able to fold the screen back and write on it to mark up documents. (Here are my thoughts on the different “Active Pens” available for it.)
However, the machine itself seems to have a hardware problem with the Thunderbolt / USB-C ports (fine for power, very flaky for data), and so I might need to replace it. And I have no idea what laptop I might get.
- Email (MUA): Thunderbird and mutt, depending on what I am doing 
  - Quick Folder Move and Move Message And Yourself are particularly useful add-ons for Thunderbird, for rapid filing of email with keyboard shortcuts
  - Thunderbird seems to be having a problem with some recurring
calendar invitations, while ikhal is handling them fine.
I’ve not dug into it, but it is a nuisance.
- Document production: LibreOffice’s Writer if I
want a “Word” document (e.g. I am exchanging documents with other
lawyers), and I am using
pandoc and typst to convert
Markdown files that I tend to write in vim for some
other bits. I’d like to explore tex at some point, as I
have a client who likes diffs to their .tex files, and that looks rather
neat.
- Presentations: reveal.js

Port 0: The Port That Does Not Exist
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-07
09:38:26 UTC
18:38:26 KST

Port 0 is the port you are not supposed to use, so nobody looks there. So we put an IRC network on it. Then I got curious whether anyone else had, and scanned all of IPv4 for port 0. Turns out we weren't alone, and some of our neighbours really shouldn't be there.
Ask any program to listen on port 0 and it will hand you a socket on port 47193...or 58037...or whatever the kernel felt like that day. Port 0 is not a port you can have. It is the number you pass when you want the operating system to pick one for you.
But a TCP header is just bytes. The destination port field is 16 bits wide, and 0x0000 fits in 16 bits as well as anything else does. Nothing on the wire stops a packet from being addressed to port 0. Your tools will refuse it, your browser will block it, and your scanner's default port list skips it, because everyone agrees it isn't a real port.
Which makes it a pretty good place to hide things, whether anyone meant to or not. So we did some TCP cowboy shit & port scanned the entire fucking internet for open ports on port 0, which uncovered some interesting unexpected findings.
The standards are thin on this. RFC 9293, the 2022 consolidation of the TCP spec, defines the source and destination port as "16 bits" and leaves it there. It has no rule against zero.
IANA lists 0/tcp and 0/udp as Reserved, pointing at RFC 6335. That RFC splits the port space into System (0–1023), User (1024–49151) and Dynamic (49152–65535) ranges, and explains what "Reserved" means:
Reserved port numbers include values at the edges of each range, e.g., 0, 1023, 1024, etc., which may be used to extend these ranges or the overall port number space in the future.
So port 0 is not forbidden. It is set aside. The only place zero has a defined protocol meaning is UDP: RFC 768 makes the source port optional, and "if not used, a value of zero is inserted." TCP has nothing like that.
Port 0's reputation comes from the sockets API, not the wire. When you call bind() with sin_port = 0, you are not asking for port 0. You are asking for any port. The ip(7) man page lists every case where Linux hands out an ephemeral port: bind() with port 0, listen() on an unbound socket, connect() on an unbound socket, and sendto() on an unbound datagram socket.
The range comes from net.ipv4.ip_local_port_range, which defaults to 32768 60999 on Linux, not the 49152–65535 that IANA calls Dynamic. And asking for port 0 needs no privileges. __inet_bind() only checks CAP_NET_BIND_SERVICE when the requested port is non-zero.
How the kernel picks is more interesting than I expected. In net/ipv4/inet_connection_sock.c (v6.8), inet_csk_find_open_port() starts at a random offset and then does this:
offset = get_random_u32_below(remaining);
/* __inet_hash_connect() favors ports having @low parity
 * We do the opposite to not pollute connect() users.
 */
offset |= 1U;
It walks the range in steps of two. The function above it says it outright: "We try to allocate an odd port (and leave even ports for connect())." Outgoing connections go through __inet_hash_connect() in inet_hashtables.c, which implements RFC 6056 Algorithm 4 (double-hash port selection) and takes the even ports first. The kernel splits the range by parity so servers asking for "any port" and clients making outbound connections don't fight over the same numbers. If SO_REUSEADDR is set, bind() also tries the lower half of the range before the upper half.
That is easy to test. On a stock Ubuntu box running Linux 6.8:
So when your program says "listen on port 0", the kernel hears "give me an odd number between 32768 and 61000." No normal program can listen on port 0.
You can bypass all of this with raw sockets, but if you just want to cheat, you can simply forward the port to another with iptables/nftables. As a fun proof of concept, I set up my IRC network at irc.supernets.org to accept plaintext connections on port 0 and SSL connections on port 1. JUST TO FLEX. 😎
-A PREROUTING -p tcp -m tcp --dport 0 -j REDIRECT --to-ports 6667
-A PREROUTING -p tcp -m tcp --dport 1 -j REDIRECT --to-ports 6697
A SYN arrives for port 0, netfilter rewrites the destination to 6667 where the ircd is actually listening, and the reply is rewritten back on the way out. The ircd never knows. Port 1 does the same for TLS on 6697.
Getting a server onto port 0 was the easy part. Getting a client there is where tools give up. I pointed everything I had at our own server on port 0:
That last one is strange, because the TCP connection underneath it is fine. The problem is that the kernel says it isn't, and it does that on purpose. Here is inet_getname() in net/ipv4/af_inet.c, which backs getpeername():
if (peer) {
	if (!inet->inet_dport ||
	    (((1 << sk->sk_state) & (TCPF_CLOSE | TCPF_SYN_SENT)) &&
	     peer == 1)) {
		release_sock(sk);
		return -ENOTCONN;
	}
Linux uses "destination port is 0" as its internal marker for "this socket isn't connected to anything." A socket that really is connected to port 0 trips that check, so getpeername() returns ENOTCONN on a working connection.
Python's ssl.wrap_socket() calls getpeername() to decide whether the socket is connected, gets ENOTCONN, decides it isn't, switches to non-blocking and tries a recv(1), gets EAGAIN, and raises BlockingIOError. The TCP connection is fine. It just reports that it doesn't exist. Python's asyncio does TLS through memory buffers instead of wrap_socket(), never asks, and works.
You can't scan port 0 with connect()-based tools for all the reasons above, but masscan doesn't use the kernel's TCP stack. It builds its own SYN packets, so port 0 is just another number.
1,173,626 addresses answered with a SYN-ACK.
That sounds like a lot of people using port 0. It isn't. Grouped by /24, the responses are packed into a few thousand dense blocks:
8% of those /24s hold 78% of the responding IPs. Whole blocks answer on every address: 172.65.0.0/16 returned 65,534 of 65,536. That isn't 65,534 servers. It's a firewall or router at the edge answering the SYN for everything behind it. 1,116,675 addresses completed the handshake and never sent a single byte.
56,951 addresses did send something. Almost all of it looked like this:
BIG-IP: [0x31dbae9:14718] {peer} TCP retransmit timeout
BIG-IP: [0x31dbae9:4992] {peer} TCP RST from remote system
BIG-IP: [0x2bd2afd:5226] SYN timeout (server not responding)
F5 BIG-IP load balancers write the reason for a reset into the RST packet, and masscan saved it as a banner. That's 53,741 of the 56,951 (94%), and 46,583 of them are in 132.246.0.0/16 alone. Drop those, plus 305 hosts returning the same fixed (Ref.Id: ?...?) token, and 2,905 addresses are left. That's about 0.25% of everything that answered.
Nobody is running anything exotic on port 0. It's the same stuff that's on every other port: SSH, databases, telnet, FTP. What stands out is how much of it is embedded gear. dropbear, micro_httpd, NAS certificates from QNAP, Synology and Buffalo, DVRs, power switches. Some of it clearly comes from one device model rolled out across an ISP:
login: prompt. The other 13 print a CLI or shell error instead.SSH-2.0-dropbear_0.48.AMG1001-T10A FTP version 1.0, with clocks set to early 2000 or 2036.MySQL 4.1.22-community-nt, an ancient Windows MySQL build.
My guess is that nobody at those ISPs picked port 0 on purpose: a port-forward or NAT rule shipped in a CPE config with the external port left at zero, and the device honored it. Then there are the hosting providers. GigaCloud (Ukraine), VDCBY (Belarus) and SiS Distribution (Thailand) have SSH on port 0 with 18, 13 and 10 different SSH version strings. Different builds means different machines, so these are individual customers, not one firmware image.
GigaCloud also accounts for 16 of the 80 mail servers on port 0, mostly Ukrainian organisations: mail.dtek-kem.com.ua (DTEK, the Ukrainian energy company), smtp01.health24.ua, mx.eastenergy.com.ua, mail.citrus.com.ua, and two government servers, mail.depfin.dn.gov.ua and mail.sed-rada.gov.ua. Why they all share this setup, I don't know. Elsewhere in mail: a Uruguayan government host, prod-carbonium01.rionegro.gub.uy, and mssante.elsan.care (62.106.147.123), a submission server for MSSanté, the French national secure health messaging system, run by the private hospital group Elsan. When masscan poked it, it replied:
220 mssante.elsan.care MSSPRO LPS AUTH_CLI submission server
501 2.7.0 Error: I can break rules, too. Goodbye.
Weird...
The rest of the router logins look the same as they would on port 80. A D-Link DSL-2740B (37.103.36.12 and two others), with "admin" already filled in as the username: two in French at Orange and Nerim in France, one in English at Wind Tre in Italy. A Linksys "Router Access" page in El Salvador (200.31.162.80). A plain "Sign in" form titled "Router Admin" on seven cloud servers: four at Hetzner, two at Online SAS, one at DigitalOcean.
One didn't bother with a login at all. A 3G/4G "Wireless Mobile Router" on China Unicom (183.93.33.155) serves its full status page on port 0 to anyone who asks: its WAN IP, LAN, WAN and wireless MAC addresses, SSID, firmware build, uptime, memory usage and whether DHCP is on.
Browsers won't open a URL with :0 in it, so to see the web interfaces I ran a small relay: listen on a local port, connect out to the target on port 0, pipe the bytes through, and point headless Chromium at the local port. 160 addresses speak HTTP on port 0, and 85 rendered a page.
A Gira HomeServer 4 (46.186.48.86), a KNX home and building automation controller, with its start page, debug page and monitor links sitting on port 0.
A Trane Tracer Synchrony login (201.171.202.25), which is HVAC and building control.
The web pages were the easy part to read. The more interesting devices don't speak HTTP. They send binary the moment you connect, and figuring out what they are meant matching bytes against nmap's probe database, Wireshark dissectors and, in one case, a disassembled vendor client.
A Beckhoff PLC with its screen open to anyone. 46.178.29.102, on Proximus in Belgium, sends this on connect:
30 01 00 00 05 6d 6d 6a 56 05 00 00 00 03 00 00 ...  "CBx063" ... "CX5120" ...
The CX5120 is a Beckhoff embedded PC running Windows CE, the kind that runs TwinCAT and drives a machine. The protocol is CERDISP, Windows CE Remote Display, which Beckhoff's own security guideline lists as open on TCP 987 by default. I disassembled Beckhoff's client, CERHOST.exe 2.0.4.0. It reads a 0x130-byte info block first, which is the 30 01 00 00 at the start of the banner. Then it checks a magic word: 0x6a6d6d05 means no password, 0x6a6d6d06 means password required. This one sends 05 6d 6d 6a. Then a 1366×768 resolution (56 05, 00 03) and screen update data. CERHOST also sends mouse and keyboard input. So this is a PLC's screen that anyone can watch and operate, with no password, on port 0.
An Omron PLC that was too busy to talk. 194.49.59.232 (AireNetworks, Spain) answered with an Omron FINS/TCP frame:
46 49 4e 53 00 00 00 10 00 00 00 01 00 00 00 20 00 00 00 00 00 00 00 66
F  I  N  S  length=16   cmd=1       error=0x20               server node 0x66
Command 1 is the server's Node Address Data Send, and error code 0x20 is "All connections are in use." It is a FINS endpoint, the protocol used to read and write Omron PLC memory, and it turned us away only because its connection slots were full. Five days later it said the same thing. A second host, 14.49.206.64 (KT, Korea), repeats an Omron Host Link frame, @FFF07F000000018C3D*, over and over. The XOR of everything before 3D is 0x3D, so the checksum is valid, but unit FF is outside the normal 00–31 range. The framing is Omron. The device behind it I couldn't pin down.
A Modbus master. 155.93.146.109 (South Africa) doesn't wait to be polled. It polls you: 10 04 00 00 00 02 72 8a is slave 16, Read Input Registers 0–1, with a valid CRC-16/MODBUS. Something out there is a SCADA master on port 0 asking whoever connects for two registers. Another host, 85.198.27.114 (Iran), repeats a 7e-framed 7-byte payload with a valid Modbus CRC, which looks like a serial-to-TCP bridge.
A power quality meter. 166.169.193.134 on Verizon Wireless answers telnet with SEL-735 Telnet Server. The SEL-735 is a Schweitzer Engineering Laboratories revenue and power quality meter, the kind utilities put on substations and feeders. This one is on a cellular modem.
Factory telemetry. 175.202.128.103 (KT, Korea) sends an HTTP POST at whoever connects:
POST /highnet/v1/POST/MachineStatus?StationCode=20209263 HTTP/1.0
host: 52.83.191.199
User-Agent: uIP/1.0 (http://www.kq-china.net/)
Data={"StationCode":"20209263","PickCard":[{"PickCardCode":"21032380","StartTime":"2026-10-7 10:3:41","MachineStatus":"0",...
kq-china.net is Zhejiang Keqiang Intelligent Control, which makes monitoring systems for injection moulding machines. uIP is Adam Dunkels' tiny embedded TCP/IP stack. A collector on a factory floor is pushing per-machine status to whoever connects, and masscan, confused, filed it as Minecraft.
A Swedish alarm. 82.183.26.214 (Telenor, Sweden) sends an STX/ETX frame containing "Kommunikationslarm medelvärde", Swedish for "communication alarm, mean value." I don't know the device.
Burglar alarm communicators. Five hosts, three in Mexico and two in Spain, send a greeting of about 56 bytes, with a 2-byte length prefix up front. That matches the README of DSC-TLink, a reverse-engineered client for DSC's alarm communicators: "opening a TCP connection with the TL280 on port 3062. The TL280 will immediately send a packet of 56 bytes." The TL280 family is the IP/cellular communicator on DSC PowerSeries Neo alarm panels, and this is the DLS path used to program and monitor the panel. Here's 213.181.95.33:
00 37 01 00 00 7e 00 30 e0 05 05 05 05 00 27 03 2b 54 03 00 02 0a 89 34 04 26 21 05
05 04 41 9f 03 00 00 50 03 02 01 03 00 00 02 04 05 06 00 03 4f 62 06 0e ea 79 06 6a 7f
(The 0a before the ICCID came out of the scan as 20, because our banner storage squashed whitespace bytes into spaces. I put it back.) The CRC-16 over the 42 bytes starting at 05 05 05 00 27 (polynomial 0xD003, from DSC-TLink's DLSProNetHeader.cs) comes out 0xea79, which is the ea 79 that follows. The MAC starts 00:03:4F, the IEEE OUI for Sur-Gard Security, DSC's receiver brand. And the device ID field on the cellular models is the SIM card's ICCID:
All three ICCIDs pass the Luhn check, and their prefixes say where the SIMs are from: 89 34 is Spain, 89 52 is Mexico. According to DSC-TLink, the AES key for the rest of the session is built from data in this greeting.
Alarm communicators that tell you the password. Three Spanish hosts, 109.167.87.236, 85.87.152.80 and 88.3.213.39, run telnet on an Alwon E20, a Notifier/Honeywell IP/GPRS alarm communicator module:
Alwon E20 Telnet Input password (defaul 1234):
Notifier's manual agrees: telnet password, "1234 por defecto." I didn't try it.
Time clocks. 187.235.160.47 and one other host at UNINET in Mexico repeat a5 00 00 00 00 7f 00 00 e0 5c. That's an Anviz time-and-attendance frame: a5, a 4-byte device ID of 0, command 7f, zero length, and a CRC-16/MCRF4XX of 0x5ce0 sent low byte first. Open-source Anviz SDKs call 0x7f a ping.
Home automation. Five hosts answer *#*1##, the OpenWebNet ACK used by BTicino and Legrand home automation gateways: 190.113.106.159 (Costa Rica), 171.242.179.164 (Vietnam), 187.145.124.63 (Mexico), 105.157.199.125 (Morocco) and 213.7.222.134 (Cyprus). 219.241.160.119 in Korea is a SiHAS HCM-300 Ver 2.12 > prompt, a Shina System smart heating thermostat. And 192.119.63.143 (wilhelm.tel, Germany) is a mediola a.i.o. Gateway that sends its encrypted cloud request to whoever connects: POST /ra/d HTTP/1.1 host: mediola-ws.elasticbeanstalk.com.
A debug menu. Three hosts on SK Telecom in Korea, 42.42.150.116, 42.42.203.12 and 42.42.201.80, greet you with this:
##################################
# DEBUG LOGIN!!
# F/W Version : EBH0920.0
# Dev ID : ldg4839
# IP : 192.168.225.100
##################################
###### MENU DISPLAY ######
l : Log On/Off.  n : NTP Query.  d : DNS Query.  i : SysEnv Informaton.
t : Time Display.  s : Server Data Send.  h : Menu Display.
192.168.225.x is the default subnet on Qualcomm LTE modules like the Quectel EC2x, so it's probably a cellular IoT device. Which product, I don't know.
A power strip. 64.201.180.254 (Gatewest, Canada) runs a Remote Power Controller v1.41i that prints its whole outlet table, circuit, name, status and boot delay, before asking for a login. Others ask only for a password but label themselves first: MGTN CO - Right Rack - Bottom, Power Switch, 151 Front Street.
SAProuters leaking their build. Four hosts are SAProuter, the SAP gateway that relays connections into SAP systems. They accepted the connection, waited 5 seconds for a route request that never came, and then sent back an NI_RTERR error packet with a lot of detail:
NI_RTERR ... *ERR* connection timed out -5 NI (network interface) 754 40
D:/depot/bas/754_REL/src/base/ni/nirout.cpp 8213
RTPENDLIST::timeoutPend: no route received within 5s (CONNECTED)
Tue Oct 6 10:56:27 2026 ... SAProuter 40.4 on 'ADSJ'
Every one gives away the SAProuter version (40.4), the SAP kernel release, the router's hostname, the server's local clock, and whether it's a Windows build (D:/) or not.
RustDesk. Four hosts, 93.118.140.111, 78.38.90.162 (Iran), 39.48.42.201 and 103.213.111.82 (Pakistan), open with HJ. That isn't a magic string. In RustDesk's bytes_codec.rs, the low two bits of the first byte give the header size and the rest gives the length, so 0x48 means an 18-byte message follows. 0x4a is protobuf field 9, Hash, which carries a salt and a challenge:
48 | 4a 10 | 0a 06 "usjk79" | 12 06 "zrktdr"
len  Hash   salt             challenge
The salt stays the same per host. The challenge is six random characters, new on every connection. That's the password challenge a RustDesk client answers before it gets the desktop. 103.213.111.82 sends a 32-character salt, which is what newer versions use.
Winflector. Eight hosts run Winflector, a Polish remote application server. 193.32.180.51 sends Terminal client version too old - please update to version 4.0.0.1 and then an RSA public key in DER form, with exponent 17, which is Crypto++'s default. The versions are sent byte-reversed: 04 03 00 04 is 4.0.3.4. All six versions I saw are on Winflector's own version list. The hosts are in Cape Verde (156.238.40.243, 156.238.47.54 and one more), Malaysia (202.187.2.203 and one more), Poland (193.32.180.51), Ghana (80.87.66.51) and India (49.204.146.227).
VNC with no password. Of the 39 VNC servers, one doesn't ask for anything. 107.174.67.223 answers with security type none and a desktop name of resolv.moe, meow meow meow :3 haiiii!!, and an 850×591 framebuffer. Four more just send RFB 005.000.
Phone switches. 117.236.74.58 (BSNL, India) is Asterisk Call Manager/2.10.5, the Asterisk Manager Interface from Asterisk 13.26–13.34. Once logged in, AMI can originate calls and run console commands. 38.229.159.25 (France) and 41.60.171.20 (Liquid) send 04 00 fb ff LAPK, the 3CX tunnel protocol.
Cameras and NAS. exacqVision video surveillance servers on 8 hosts, including two at OneCleveland (204.156.216.130 and 204.156.198.238), send a 56-byte header ending in three values that change on every connection. Six QNAP NAS boxes expose RTRR (Realtime Remote Replication, ab ca a5 5d ...), on 202.184.138.61 in Malaysia and five others in Austria, Sri Lanka, Spain and Italy. Two Rifatron DVRs send RIFA, 42.200.149.78 in Hong Kong and 14.241.231.170 in Vietnam. One more is a UMS WebViewer DVR stream in Japan (123.242.213.10).
The rest of the RPC zoo. 223.76.20.55 (China Mobile) is ZeroC Ice, IceP 1.0 Validate Connection. 191.13.74.178 (Telefonica, Brazil) sends just ac ed 00 05, the header of a Java serialization stream, and then nothing else. 119.152.239.20 (Pakistan) sends a bare HTTP/2 SETTINGS frame with the same bytes as a default grpc-go server. 212.80.175.132 (Spain) has things backwards and sends us a full TLS 1.2 ClientHello. And 116.75.83.213 (Hathway, India) responds with RP-Version: 5.0 and RP-Application-Reason: 80108bff, which is Sony PlayStation Remote Play, most likely a PS4.
21 addresses don't wait to be asked. Connect to port 0 and they start streaming comma-separated text, about 7.5 KB of it per host in our capture:
2026/09/23 14:31:49,00:00:11,0,24,O,<number>,<number>,,0,1002639,0,E24,<name>,T9031,Line 31.1,0,0,...
That's SMDR (Station Message Detail Recording) output from an Avaya IP Office phone system. The fields line up with Avaya's documented layout: call start, connected time, ring time, caller, direction, called and dialled number, a call ID that starts at 1,000,000, then the two parties as E for extension or T for trunk, with names like "Line 31.1". Each host handed over 37 to 62 calls with real phone numbers, staff names, extension numbers and internal addresses. The oldest record is from December 2018. The newest is from a few days before the scan. Several hosts start with records that are years old, which I can't explain: 94.30.72.110 opened with calls from 2018-12-21, 31.10.39.182 with 2023-09-18.
Nothing was sent to get this. IP Office pushes SMDR records to whatever connects to its SMDR port, and on these 21 hosts that port is reachable on port 0. masscan stops reading at about 7.5 KB, so that's only the first few dozen calls per host.
19 of the 21 are in the UK, on BT, Wavenet, Gamma, TalkTalk, Plusnet and a handful of smaller business ISPs. One is in the US and one in South Africa. However these ended up on port 0, anyone who connects can read the company's call history.
bind(), and a single NAT rule gets around it.nc and telnet refuse to try, Chromium blocks it, and the Linux kernel's own getpeername() reports a working port 0 connection as not connected. A lot of software assumes port 0 means "nothing is here."
Come say hi on the port that doesn't exist. If your client lets you.
- - -
Sources & References:
message.proto, connection.rs
Connect to SuperNETs on port 0 (plaintext) or port 1 (TLS) at irc.supernets.org, or reach out at acid.vegas@acid.vegas. We hang out in #war.

====================================================================================================

Port 0: The Port That Does Not Exist
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-07
09:38:26 UTC
18:38:26 KST

Port 0 is the port you are not supposed to use, so nobody looks there. So we put an IRC network on it. Then I got curious whether anyone else had, and scanned all of IPv4 for port 0. Turns out we weren't alone, and some of our neighbours really shouldn't be there.
Ask any program to listen on port 0 and it will hand you a socket on port 47193...or 58037...or whatever the kernel felt like that day. Port 0 is not a port you can have. It is the number you pass when you want the operating system to pick one for you.
But a TCP header is just bytes. The destination port field is 16 bits wide, and 0x0000 fits in 16 bits as well as anything else does. Nothing on the wire stops a packet from being addressed to port 0. Your tools will refuse it, your browser will block it, and your scanner's default port list skips it, because everyone agrees it isn't a real port.
Which makes it a pretty good place to hide things, whether anyone meant to or not. So we did some TCP cowboy shit & port scanned the entire fucking internet for open ports on port 0, which uncovered some interesting unexpected findings.
The standards are thin on this. RFC 9293, the 2022 consolidation of the TCP spec, defines the source and destination port as "16 bits" and leaves it there. It has no rule against zero.
IANA lists 0/tcp and 0/udp as Reserved, pointing at RFC 6335. That RFC splits the port space into System (0–1023), User (1024–49151) and Dynamic (49152–65535) ranges, and explains what "Reserved" means:
Reserved port numbers include values at the edges of each range, e.g., 0, 1023, 1024, etc., which may be used to extend these ranges or the overall port number space in the future.
So port 0 is not forbidden. It is set aside. The only place zero has a defined protocol meaning is UDP: RFC 768 makes the source port optional, and "if not used, a value of zero is inserted." TCP has nothing like that.
Port 0's reputation comes from the sockets API, not the wire. When you call bind() with sin_port = 0, you are not asking for port 0. You are asking for any port. The ip(7) man page lists every case where Linux hands out an ephemeral port: bind() with port 0, listen() on an unbound socket, connect() on an unbound socket, and sendto() on an unbound datagram socket.
The range comes from net.ipv4.ip_local_port_range, which defaults to 32768 60999 on Linux, not the 49152–65535 that IANA calls Dynamic. And asking for port 0 needs no privileges. __inet_bind() only checks CAP_NET_BIND_SERVICE when the requested port is non-zero.
How the kernel picks is more interesting than I expected. In net/ipv4/inet_connection_sock.c (v6.8), inet_csk_find_open_port() starts at a random offset and then does this:
offset = get_random_u32_below(remaining);
/* __inet_hash_connect() favors ports having @low parity
 * We do the opposite to not pollute connect() users.
 */
offset |= 1U;
It walks the range in steps of two. The function above it says it outright: "We try to allocate an odd port (and leave even ports for connect())." Outgoing connections go through __inet_hash_connect() in inet_hashtables.c, which implements RFC 6056 Algorithm 4 (double-hash port selection) and takes the even ports first. The kernel splits the range by parity so servers asking for "any port" and clients making outbound connections don't fight over the same numbers. If SO_REUSEADDR is set, bind() also tries the lower half of the range before the upper half.
That is easy to test. On a stock Ubuntu box running Linux 6.8:
So when your program says "listen on port 0", the kernel hears "give me an odd number between 32768 and 61000." No normal program can listen on port 0.
You can bypass all of this with raw sockets, but if you just want to cheat, you can simply forward the port to another with iptables/nftables. As a fun proof of concept, I set up my IRC network at irc.supernets.org to accept plaintext connections on port 0 and SSL connections on port 1. JUST TO FLEX. 😎
-A PREROUTING -p tcp -m tcp --dport 0 -j REDIRECT --to-ports 6667
-A PREROUTING -p tcp -m tcp --dport 1 -j REDIRECT --to-ports 6697
A SYN arrives for port 0, netfilter rewrites the destination to 6667 where the ircd is actually listening, and the reply is rewritten back on the way out. The ircd never knows. Port 1 does the same for TLS on 6697.
Getting a server onto port 0 was the easy part. Getting a client there is where tools give up. I pointed everything I had at our own server on port 0:
That last one is strange, because the TCP connection underneath it is fine. The problem is that the kernel says it isn't, and it does that on purpose. Here is inet_getname() in net/ipv4/af_inet.c, which backs getpeername():
if (peer) {
	if (!inet->inet_dport ||
	    (((1 << sk->sk_state) & (TCPF_CLOSE | TCPF_SYN_SENT)) &&
	     peer == 1)) {
		release_sock(sk);
		return -ENOTCONN;
	}
Linux uses "destination port is 0" as its internal marker for "this socket isn't connected to anything." A socket that really is connected to port 0 trips that check, so getpeername() returns ENOTCONN on a working connection.
Python's ssl.wrap_socket() calls getpeername() to decide whether the socket is connected, gets ENOTCONN, decides it isn't, switches to non-blocking and tries a recv(1), gets EAGAIN, and raises BlockingIOError. The TCP connection is fine. It just reports that it doesn't exist. Python's asyncio does TLS through memory buffers instead of wrap_socket(), never asks, and works.
You can't scan port 0 with connect()-based tools for all the reasons above, but masscan doesn't use the kernel's TCP stack. It builds its own SYN packets, so port 0 is just another number.
1,173,626 addresses answered with a SYN-ACK.
That sounds like a lot of people using port 0. It isn't. Grouped by /24, the responses are packed into a few thousand dense blocks:
8% of those /24s hold 78% of the responding IPs. Whole blocks answer on every address: 172.65.0.0/16 returned 65,534 of 65,536. That isn't 65,534 servers. It's a firewall or router at the edge answering the SYN for everything behind it. 1,116,675 addresses completed the handshake and never sent a single byte.
56,951 addresses did send something. Almost all of it looked like this:
BIG-IP: [0x31dbae9:14718] {peer} TCP retransmit timeout
BIG-IP: [0x31dbae9:4992] {peer} TCP RST from remote system
BIG-IP: [0x2bd2afd:5226] SYN timeout (server not responding)
F5 BIG-IP load balancers write the reason for a reset into the RST packet, and masscan saved it as a banner. That's 53,741 of the 56,951 (94%), and 46,583 of them are in 132.246.0.0/16 alone. Drop those, plus 305 hosts returning the same fixed (Ref.Id: ?...?) token, and 2,905 addresses are left. That's about 0.25% of everything that answered.
Nobody is running anything exotic on port 0. It's the same stuff that's on every other port: SSH, databases, telnet, FTP. What stands out is how much of it is embedded gear. dropbear, micro_httpd, NAS certificates from QNAP, Synology and Buffalo, DVRs, power switches. Some of it clearly comes from one device model rolled out across an ISP:
login: prompt. The other 13 print a CLI or shell error instead.SSH-2.0-dropbear_0.48.AMG1001-T10A FTP version 1.0, with clocks set to early 2000 or 2036.MySQL 4.1.22-community-nt, an ancient Windows MySQL build.
My guess is that nobody at those ISPs picked port 0 on purpose: a port-forward or NAT rule shipped in a CPE config with the external port left at zero, and the device honored it. Then there are the hosting providers. GigaCloud (Ukraine), VDCBY (Belarus) and SiS Distribution (Thailand) have SSH on port 0 with 18, 13 and 10 different SSH version strings. Different builds means different machines, so these are individual customers, not one firmware image.
GigaCloud also accounts for 16 of the 80 mail servers on port 0, mostly Ukrainian organisations: mail.dtek-kem.com.ua (DTEK, the Ukrainian energy company), smtp01.health24.ua, mx.eastenergy.com.ua, mail.citrus.com.ua, and two government servers, mail.depfin.dn.gov.ua and mail.sed-rada.gov.ua. Why they all share this setup, I don't know. Elsewhere in mail: a Uruguayan government host, prod-carbonium01.rionegro.gub.uy, and mssante.elsan.care (62.106.147.123), a submission server for MSSanté, the French national secure health messaging system, run by the private hospital group Elsan. When masscan poked it, it replied:
220 mssante.elsan.care MSSPRO LPS AUTH_CLI submission server
501 2.7.0 Error: I can break rules, too. Goodbye.
Weird...
The rest of the router logins look the same as they would on port 80. A D-Link DSL-2740B (37.103.36.12 and two others), with "admin" already filled in as the username: two in French at Orange and Nerim in France, one in English at Wind Tre in Italy. A Linksys "Router Access" page in El Salvador (200.31.162.80). A plain "Sign in" form titled "Router Admin" on seven cloud servers: four at Hetzner, two at Online SAS, one at DigitalOcean.
One didn't bother with a login at all. A 3G/4G "Wireless Mobile Router" on China Unicom (183.93.33.155) serves its full status page on port 0 to anyone who asks: its WAN IP, LAN, WAN and wireless MAC addresses, SSID, firmware build, uptime, memory usage and whether DHCP is on.
Browsers won't open a URL with :0 in it, so to see the web interfaces I ran a small relay: listen on a local port, connect out to the target on port 0, pipe the bytes through, and point headless Chromium at the local port. 160 addresses speak HTTP on port 0, and 85 rendered a page.
A Gira HomeServer 4 (46.186.48.86), a KNX home and building automation controller, with its start page, debug page and monitor links sitting on port 0.
A Trane Tracer Synchrony login (201.171.202.25), which is HVAC and building control.
The web pages were the easy part to read. The more interesting devices don't speak HTTP. They send binary the moment you connect, and figuring out what they are meant matching bytes against nmap's probe database, Wireshark dissectors and, in one case, a disassembled vendor client.
A Beckhoff PLC with its screen open to anyone. 46.178.29.102, on Proximus in Belgium, sends this on connect:
30 01 00 00 05 6d 6d 6a 56 05 00 00 00 03 00 00 ...  "CBx063" ... "CX5120" ...
The CX5120 is a Beckhoff embedded PC running Windows CE, the kind that runs TwinCAT and drives a machine. The protocol is CERDISP, Windows CE Remote Display, which Beckhoff's own security guideline lists as open on TCP 987 by default. I disassembled Beckhoff's client, CERHOST.exe 2.0.4.0. It reads a 0x130-byte info block first, which is the 30 01 00 00 at the start of the banner. Then it checks a magic word: 0x6a6d6d05 means no password, 0x6a6d6d06 means password required. This one sends 05 6d 6d 6a. Then a 1366×768 resolution (56 05, 00 03) and screen update data. CERHOST also sends mouse and keyboard input. So this is a PLC's screen that anyone can watch and operate, with no password, on port 0.
An Omron PLC that was too busy to talk. 194.49.59.232 (AireNetworks, Spain) answered with an Omron FINS/TCP frame:
46 49 4e 53 00 00 00 10 00 00 00 01 00 00 00 20 00 00 00 00 00 00 00 66
F  I  N  S  length=16   cmd=1       error=0x20               server node 0x66
Command 1 is the server's Node Address Data Send, and error code 0x20 is "All connections are in use." It is a FINS endpoint, the protocol used to read and write Omron PLC memory, and it turned us away only because its connection slots were full. Five days later it said the same thing. A second host, 14.49.206.64 (KT, Korea), repeats an Omron Host Link frame, @FFF07F000000018C3D*, over and over. The XOR of everything before 3D is 0x3D, so the checksum is valid, but unit FF is outside the normal 00–31 range. The framing is Omron. The device behind it I couldn't pin down.
A Modbus master. 155.93.146.109 (South Africa) doesn't wait to be polled. It polls you: 10 04 00 00 00 02 72 8a is slave 16, Read Input Registers 0–1, with a valid CRC-16/MODBUS. Something out there is a SCADA master on port 0 asking whoever connects for two registers. Another host, 85.198.27.114 (Iran), repeats a 7e-framed 7-byte payload with a valid Modbus CRC, which looks like a serial-to-TCP bridge.
A power quality meter. 166.169.193.134 on Verizon Wireless answers telnet with SEL-735 Telnet Server. The SEL-735 is a Schweitzer Engineering Laboratories revenue and power quality meter, the kind utilities put on substations and feeders. This one is on a cellular modem.
Factory telemetry. 175.202.128.103 (KT, Korea) sends an HTTP POST at whoever connects:
POST /highnet/v1/POST/MachineStatus?StationCode=20209263 HTTP/1.0
host: 52.83.191.199
User-Agent: uIP/1.0 (http://www.kq-china.net/)
Data={"StationCode":"20209263","PickCard":[{"PickCardCode":"21032380","StartTime":"2026-10-7 10:3:41","MachineStatus":"0",...

Alwon E20 Telnet Input password (defaul 1234):

Five hosts answer *#*1##, the OpenWebNet ACK used by BTicino and Legrand home automation gateways: 190.113.106.159 (Costa Rica), 171.242.179.164 (Vietnam), 187.145.124.63 (Mexico), 105.157.199.125 (Morocco) and 213.7.222.134 (Cyprus). 219.241.160.119 in Korea is a SiHAS HCM-300 Ver 2.12 > prompt, a Shina System smart heating thermostat. A debug menu. Three hosts on SK Telecom in Korea, 42.42.150.116, 42.42.203.12 and 42.42.201.80, greet you with this:

D:/depot/bas/754_REL/src/base/ni/nirout.cpp 8213
RTPENDLIST::timeoutPend: no route received within 5s (CONNECTED)
Tue Oct 6 10:56:27 2026 ... SAProuter 40.4 on 'ADSJ'
Every one gives away the SAProuter version (40.4), the SAP kernel release, the router's hostname, the server's local clock, and whether it's a Windows build (D:/) or not.
RustDesk. Four hosts, 93.118.140.111, 78.38.90.162 (Iran), 39.48.42.201 and 103.213.111.82 (Pakistan), open with HJ. That isn't a magic string. In RustDesk's bytes_codec.rs, the low two bits of the first byte give the header size and the rest gives the length, so 0x48 means an 18-byte message follows. 0x4a is protobuf field 9, Hash, which carries a salt and a challenge:
48 | 4a 10 | 0a 06 "usjk79" | 12 06 "zrktdr"
len  Hash   salt             challenge
The salt stays the same per host. The challenge is six random characters, new on every connection. That's the password challenge a RustDesk client answers before it gets the desktop. 103.213.111.82 sends a 32-character salt, which is what newer versions use.
Winflector. Eight hosts run Winflector, a Polish remote application server. 193.32.180.51 sends Terminal client version too old - please update to version 4.0.0.1 and then an RSA public key in DER form, with exponent 17, which is Crypto++'s default. The versions are sent byte-reversed: 04 03 00 04 is 4.0.3.4. All six versions I saw are on Winflector's own version list. The hosts are in Cape Verde (156.238.40.243, 156.238.47.54 and one more), Malaysia (202.187.2.203 and one more), Poland (193.32.180.51), Ghana (80.87.66.51) and India (49.204.146.227).
VNC with no password. Of the 39 VNC servers, one doesn't ask for anything. 107.174.67.223 answers with security type none and a desktop name of resolv.moe, meow meow meow :3 haiiii!!, and an 850×591 framebuffer. Four more just send RFB 005.000.
Phone switches. 117.236.74.58 (BSNL, India) is Asterisk Call Manager/2.10.5, the Asterisk Manager Interface from Asterisk 13.26–13.34. Once logged in, AMI can originate calls and run console commands. 38.229.159.25 (France) and 41.60.171.20 (Liquid) send 04 00 fb ff LAPK, the 3CX tunnel protocol.
Cameras and NAS. exacqVision video surveillance servers on 8 hosts, including two at OneCleveland (204.156.216.130 and 204.156.198.238), send a 56-byte header ending in three values that change on every connection. Six QNAP NAS boxes expose RTRR (Realtime Remote Replication, ab ca a5 5d ...), on 202.184.138.61 in Malaysia and five others in Austria, Sri Lanka, Spain and Italy. Two Rifatron DVRs send RIFA, 42.200.149.78 in Hong Kong and 14.241.231.170 in Vietnam. One more is a UMS WebViewer DVR stream in Japan (123.242.213.10).
The rest of the RPC zoo. 223.76.20.55 (China Mobile) is ZeroC Ice, IceP 1.0 Validate Connection. 191.13.74.178 (Telefonica, Brazil) sends just ac ed 00 05, the header of a Java serialization stream, and then nothing else. 119.152.239.20 (Pakistan) sends a bare HTTP/2 SETTINGS frame with the same bytes as a default grpc-go server. 212.80.175.132 (Spain) has things backwards and sends us a full TLS 1.2 ClientHello. And 116.75.83.213 (Hathway, India) responds with RP-Version: 5.0 and RP-Application-Reason: 80108bff, which is Sony PlayStation Remote Play, most likely a PS4.
21 addresses don't wait to be asked. Connect to port 0 and they start streaming comma-separated text, about 7.5 KB of it per host in our capture:
2026/09/23 14:31:49,00:00:11,0,24,O,<number>,<number>,,0,1002639,0,E24,<name>,T9031,Line 31.1,0,0,...
That's SMDR (Station Message Detail Recording) output from an Avaya IP Office phone system. The fields line up with Avaya's documented layout: call start, connected time, ring time, caller, direction, called and dialled number, a call ID that starts at 1,000,000, then the two parties as E for extension or T for trunk, with names like "Line 31.1". Each host handed over 37 to 62 calls with real phone numbers, staff names, extension numbers and internal addresses. The oldest record is from December 2018. The newest is from a few days before the scan. Several hosts start with records that are years old, which I can't explain: 94.30.72.110 opened with calls from 2018-12-21, 31.10.39.182 with 2023-09-18.
Nothing was sent to get this. IP Office pushes SMDR records to whatever connects to its SMDR port, and on these 21 hosts that port is reachable on port 0. masscan stops reading at about 7.5 KB, so that's only the first few dozen calls per host.
19 of the 21 are in the UK, on BT, Wavenet, Gamma, TalkTalk, Plusnet and a handful of smaller business ISPs. One is in the US and one in South Africa. However these ended up on port 0, anyone who connects can read the company's call history.
bind(), and a single NAT rule gets around it.nc and telnet refuse to try, Chromium blocks it, and the Linux kernel's own getpeername() reports a working port 0 connection as not connected. A lot of software assumes port 0 means "nothing is here."
Come say hi on the port that doesn't exist. If your client lets you.
