Vibrating Mattress Helps Clear Alzheimer's Protein From the Brain
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-07
07:00:00 UTC
16:00:00 KST

Vibrating Mattress Helps Clear Alzheimer's Protein From the Brain 29
			
		 	
				A vibrating mattress combined with two pills that release nitric oxide enhanced the brain's ability to clear an Alzheimer's-related protein. According to New Scientist, the experiment "caused the brain's blood vessels to swell and constrict more strongly, which enhanced the glymphatic system's clearance of [the protein]." From the report:  [Kesa Kiviniemi at the University of Oulu in Finland] and his colleagues recruited 29 adults who hadn't been diagnosed with Alzheimer's disease or any other cognitive issues. The participants took a pill that dissolves when under the tongue, releasing nitric oxide. They then lay on a mattress that caused their bodies to gently vibrate for 40 minutes, taking another pill halfway through. Blood samples revealed that their levels of a type of protein called beta-amyloid 42 increased by 35 per cent, on average, after the intervention. This protein, which usually helps neurons signal and connect to each other, misfolds and form clumps in Alzheimer's disease.
"The treatment seemed to enhance the glymphatic system's ability to clear beta-amyloid from the brain to the blood," says Kiviniemi. "A 35 per cent increase in circulating beta-amyloid 42 is a substantial and very interesting acute effect," says Tuomas Lilius at the University of Helsinki, who wasn't involved in the work. "It is certainly possible it could become biologically, and eventually clinically, relevant." Side effects were mild and minimal, with six of the participants experiencing issues such as nausea and headache. However, this could partly be due to the expectation of an effect from the nitric oxide pills. The researchers hope to overcome this in future work by including placebo pills.
Brain scans suggest the treatment enhanced the flow of fluid along glymphatic channels, probably by widening the brain's blood vessels and making them pump more strongly, says Kiviniemi. But other mechanisms could also be at play, says Brown. For instance, this intervention may slow the breakdown of beta-amyloid 42 by the liver, leading to higher amounts in the bloodstream, he says. Future studies could get around this by injecting markers into cerebrospinal fluid, which moves through the glymphatic system, to more directly measure its flow, says Brown. Additional experiments by Kiviniemi and his colleagues tested either only the mattress vibrations or only the nitric oxide pills on the same participants, at least a week before or after they underwent the combined approach. Based on brain scans, these solo interventions seemed to have less than half the effect on the glymphatic system than when they were combined. The findings have been published in the journal Nature Communications.

The Components of Agent Memory
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-07
06:39:04 UTC
15:39:04 KST

Søren Kierkegaard wrote that life can only be understood backward, but it must be lived forward. We’ve encountered a similar bind when designing memory for AI agents.
As humans, we encode memory constantly as we go about our lives. The hippocampus plays a central role in this, capturing experiences in the moment so that memories form quickly and are immediately available. But only some experiences are consolidated into long-term knowledge.
Much of that consolidation appears to happen when our brains are offline. As we sleep, and during quiet rest, the hippocampus replays our recent experiences. This is thought to gradually distill those experiences and integrate them into the neocortex—the part of our brains that stores most of what we think of as knowledge.
Agent memory can use a similar division of labor. Agents capture much of their work in logs or transcripts. Researchers at Letta and UC Berkeley proposed sleep-time compute as a way for models to use idle time between interactions to reorganize their existing context. The same idea can help agents organize their memory by reflecting on logs to fix mistakes or produce new notes and representations. This process is often called dreaming (Figure 1).
We’ve found that dreaming has been a useful tool for helping us give Claude effective memory. In this post, we share how we’ve come to think about agent memory and walk through several evaluations we’ve used to explore agent memory, including the use of dreaming, and what each component contributes to memory organization.
Memory notes
By “agent memory,” we mean knowledge that Claude gains from experience—including interactions with users and tasks it’s been assigned—and that it retains between sessions, saved as context it can read later. It excludes other ways models can learn or manage context, such as changes to their weights (the internal parameters set during training), in-context learning (knowledge gained within a single conversation), and compaction (summaries written by Claude that replace its older turns so it can continue working in a new context window).
In the human brain, chemical signals released before, during, or after an experience can mark a memory as important. Marked memories tend to be replayed more often than unmarked ones during offline periods. This process, called replay, helps stabilize and integrate them into long-term memory.
Similarly, Claude can tag salient events in a session log as they happen, so they’re easier to find later, or it can write them to a memory store, such as a folder of memory notes (Figure 2). But how can Claude tell what’s salient in the moment, before it knows what’s coming next?
Memory can be a prediction about what the future will need
Memory is, occasionally, a prediction. By writing down a rule, an agent predicts that the pattern it’s describing will be relevant again. For instance, writing down a verified fact is a prediction that an agent in a future session will want to rely on that fact without double-checking it. Over time, Claude has become much better at making these kinds of predictions.
Consider a task from Continual Learning Bench (CL-Bench) 1.0, a benchmark that measures an agent’s ability to use memory in order to tackle long-horizon tasks. One particular task asks Claude to answer a series of questions by looking up information in a database. After Claude gives an answer, it’s told the correct solution and can write a memory to help answer future questions.
Memory can support a learning loop here. If Claude gets a question wrong, it can record the failure, come up with theories about what went wrong, test those theories, and turn what it finds into a rule about how to answer in the future.
We ran the CL-Bench 1.0 task using Claude Managed Agents. Claude started each question in a fresh session, with one exception: it could retrieve files from a memory directory that persisted from one session to the next.
Different models achieved different levels of success at creating their own learning loops. Claude Sonnet 4.6 got as far as generating theories about why an answer was wrong. Its memory directory contained a scattering of files, each listing several untested guesses.
Claude Opus 4.7 consolidated its notes into a single reference document and began marking which facts it had checked and which were still unconfirmed. Claude Sonnet 5 marked each answer as confirmed after the grader had checked it, and wrote a correction when it had answered wrong.
Claude Opus 5 was the first of these models to close the loop. Most of its entries included a rule (and also how it knew the rule was true), answers to earlier questions, and clear warnings about traps it had discovered along the way (Figure 3).
Like Opus 5, Claude Opus 5.5 and Claude Sonnet 5.5 both closed the loop, using memory to capture validated rules about how to best answer future questions. With memory, both scored about the same as Opus 5, though their baseline performance without memory was slightly higher.
Claude has learned to judge salience
We also evaluated Claude’s ability to play Pokémon Red, a game that challenges Claude to navigate complex long-horizon tasks, as a test of Claude’s judgment about what to save to memory. At the start of each run, we gave each model a folder of memory files that it could read from and write to while it played (Figure 4).
We compared the memory folders from one run each of Claude Sonnet 3.7 and Claude Fable 5, when each was about 10,000 steps into the game. A key difference we noticed was what each model thought was worth remembering. Sonnet 3.7 assumed everything might matter and wrote to memory indiscriminately. Its memory folder contained an archive of encounters, often with a separate file for each battle it fought, each item it found, and each non-player character it met. Ultimately, Sonnet 3.7 wrote 413 memory files across 225 folders and earned one badge (badges mark a player’s progress through the game).
Fable 5, by comparison, wrote nine files in two folders, plus a single top-level file called global.md, and earned four badges.
Sonnet 3.7’s approach was a problem mainly because the game state of Pokémon changes constantly—Sonnet 3.7’s detailed record was continuously going out of date. As a result, as Claude progressed, it had to spend more time and resources combing through its memories rather than playing the game. Around step 10,000, for example, Sonnet 3.7 started its fourth folder of “master” memory files. Over the next 17 steps, it created six files, deleted nine others, and took no actions in the game itself.
Fable 5’s approach used memory for things the game didn’t explicitly tell it. Some of the kinds of information Sonnet 3.7 wrote to memory—such as how much money the player has or where the player is on the map—can be read off the screen every turn, so storing it is redundant. By contrast, memory files about strategy, plans, and tested rules exist only if Claude works them out, and they disappear unless Claude writes them down.
Fable 5 organized its memory accordingly. Its global.md file acted as a dashboard, holding the current goal, a prewritten plan for tackling gyms (the challenges where badges are won), and the model’s own rules for using memory. A locations folder held plans for navigating particular routes, and a gameplay folder held strategies based on what it had learned about how the game works. For example, after seven Poké Balls in a row failed to catch a Magikarp (one of the easiest Pokémon to catch), Claude wrote, “If 3+ balls fail in a row, VARY the timing (insert extra ‘wait’ presses between menu steps) - the catch landed right after doing this.”
With a much smaller memory folder, Fable 5 progressed much further in the game than Sonnet 3.7. Opus 5.5 took a similar approach: it kept a small folder of memory notes that tracked its current goal and next steps, rewrote them as it played, and beat the game.
Steering Claude to write memory
We’ve found that it helps to tell Claude what memory is for, but not how to use it. On CL-Bench, we simply told Claude that a memory folder was available. That general guidance was enough for Opus 4.7 and Opus 5 to use memory effectively, and it gave both a noticeable boost in performance (Figure 3).
We also tested giving the models specific rules about what they should write to memory, with one rule per test. We compared how much each model gained from memory with each rule against how much it gained without it. When we set a rule to “record only failures,” both Opus 5 and Opus 4.7 lost much of the performance boost that memory gave them (Figure 5, right). For stronger models, giving them freedom to judge salience was more effective than setting fixed rules in advance, and those rules could end up weakening performance.
We also noticed that steering helped older models the most. For instance, Sonnet 4.6 often didn’t read files from its memory directory and failed to test its theories about why an answer was wrong.
In this case, prompting Claude to read its memory files before answering—and then to explicitly test its theories—nearly doubled its benchmark score (Figure 5, left). Sonnet 5 does this on its own. Without being prompted to, it read its memory before answering 9 out of 10 times. By comparison, Sonnet 4.6 did this less than half of the time.
With frontier Claude models, we typically give simple guidance to create memory notes when a person asks them to or when something is clearly worth remembering, and we leave that judgment up to Claude.
Dreaming can correct memory
Since Claude writes to memory based on what it judges to be salient in-session, a note that’s wrong from the start or that drifts as the environment changes can be read, and trusted, by Claude in a later session.
Dreaming gives Claude a way to test its notes against the original transcripts (Figure 6). We saw a positive example of this when Claude played Pokémon using Managed Agents with a mounted directory for its memories.
In one part of the game, the player has to get through Mt. Moon, a cave system with its entrance on the west side of the map and its exit on the east. Near the entrance is a trap: a one-way ledge that drops the player onto the route they came from, with no clear way to climb back up. Claude was using the PokéCenter, a building at the mountain’s entrance, as a landmark, so whether it walked off the ledge depended on which side of the mountain it thought the PokéCenter was on.
At one point, Claude wrote down (incorrectly) that the PokéCenter was on the east side. When it walked out of the PokéCenter, it announced, “Mt Moon is behind me,” and updated its notes: “Mt Moon DONE.” But Mt. Moon was not behind it. A few actions later, Claude went over the ledge and landed back on the route it had come from (Figure 7). Its navigation tool didn’t show the drop, so Claude concluded, “I overshot far west,” and that it had been on the correct side all along. It hadn’t.
We then tested whether dreaming could help Claude avoid this trap. We ran dreaming on the memory folder that contained the wrong location for the PokéCenter: Claude checked the note against the session transcript, caught its error, and rewrote it. The transcript gave Claude a ground truth to validate its memory against. With the corrected memory note, Claude went straight back into Mt. Moon and eventually reached Cerulean City.
In the run with the uncorrected note, Claude dropped back onto that earlier route three times. After the third failure, Claude seemed to give up, writing, “I’m going to stop rather than keep looping.” Our setup’s automatic reply instructed it to continue. So it did, all the way back to Pewter City, the town before the mountain, where it hoped to heal and restock. The run ended without Claude ever reaching Cerulean City.
Dreaming can build new memory representations
With dreaming, Claude can build representations to help navigate memory (Figure 9). One type of memory representation it uses is called a wiki, a collection of pages written and maintained by Claude that gathers what Claude knows about one subject and links it to other notes. These links effectively provide Claude with a map it can use to sift through its memory and efficiently locate memories when they’re needed.
Representations like wikis can help Claude traverse its ever-growing knowledge base. We explored the benefit of wikis as a memory representation using a corpus of internal documents—about 350,000 files, roughly 420M tokens—from Stainless, a software company we acquired in May. We created an evaluation set of 25 questions about this corpus and gave Claude a search tool to explore it. We also instructed Claude to build a wiki based on these files using dreaming. Over 128 sessions, we prompted Opus 5 agents to explore the files, write or correct linked wiki pages, merge duplicates, and check claims against the sources.
Each session built on the progress that agents made in prior sessions, using a shared log to coordinate work. As we scaled dreaming from two sessions to 128, performance on the 25 questions grew from 13% to 48% of graded criteria (Figure 10).
Claude builds its wikis incrementally. After creating an initial wiki, it uses subsequent rounds of dreaming to add to, edit, or prune it. In the early rounds of dreaming, Claude’s knowledge is like that of a new employee, and its ability to answer questions based on the wiki alone is limited. The more it dreams, the richer its knowledge map grows, like that of a seasoned employee. In our tests, we found that the wiki helped Claude the most when we also gave it access to a search tool. When it used both search and the wiki, Claude’s aggregate score rose from 54% to 61% as that wiki grew, compared with 53% when it had access to the search tool alone. Claude’s best score was 48% when it used only the wiki to answer questions. Using search and the wiki also saved about 20,000 tokens per question.
We wanted to test which kinds of tasks benefited from Claude having a wiki. To do this, we used the Harvey LAB benchmark along with a collection of documents about a merger. The benchmark gives Claude a set of documents and asks Claude to generate a legal brief based on their content. The benchmark then grades the brief against a list of criteria. While a tad less engaging, perhaps, than a Pokémon run, these evaluations give us diverse collections of documents to build wikis from.
We instructed Claude to build a wiki from the documents. We then asked Claude to answer questions (about the merger) or generate legal briefs (the LAB benchmark) both from the wiki and from a direct file search. One test asked 24 questions about the merger (Figure 11, left), more than half of which needed Claude to find information across several documents, and many of which also needed Claude to use judgment or do math. On those items (red and orange lines), Claude scored 87% when it consulted the wiki, compared with 73% when it used search alone.
In other cases, the wiki was less useful. On one LAB task (Figure 11, right), brief criteria often could be retrieved with a text search from a single document, and the wiki showed no clear gain in performance relative to search. On another LAB task, the wiki grew larger than the documents themselves (Figure 12). Like the mapmakers in Borges’s one-paragraph short story “On Exactitude in Science,” who drafted a map so detailed it was as large as the empire it represented, Claude had made an extremely detailed wiki that effectively abstracted nothing.
Dreaming can promote notes up the memory hierarchy
Dreaming can also float notes to the top of the memory hierarchy, and the agent harness can preemptively load them into Claude’s context at the start of every session (Figure 13). This means Claude doesn’t need to search for them, or even to know there’s something to search for.
Promoting memories into a new session (a form of context injection) must be done carefully. Injected memories use up valuable tokens, and an incorrect note can be carried into every session. Loading a note also doesn’t guarantee that Claude acts on it: our documentation on Claude Code warns that longer instruction files reduce adherence. So the top layer of the memory hierarchy has to stay relatively small, and much of dreaming’s work here is keeping it that way—by moving some details down the hierarchy and leaving links behind.
Claude Code’s auto memory keeps its injected layer small in the same way, with Claude maintaining it during the session. Claude doesn’t load all of its auto memory into every session’s context window. It loads only the first 200 lines or 25 KB of MEMORY.md. This header serves as an index, which shows Claude what it knows and where to find it.
Dreaming can focus memory on a specific goal
Dreaming can also shape how memory grows. An assistant like Claude Duet, for example, often benefits from having memories that are organized around the person it’s helping, and dreaming can be tuned to build memory in service of a particular goal.
The instructions that guide how memory grows differ by task and tell Claude what information actually matters. For example, one task in the LAB benchmark simulates a company acquisition and provides a set of contracts. The brief is graded on whether it correctly describes the contract terms.
We started with basic instructions for generating the wiki that didn’t tell Claude how to use the wiki. This task relied heavily on contract terms. While the wiki was being built, several agents wrote to a shared table of contract terms at the same time (Figure 14, left).
The result was a wiki that was missing key facts. When we graded a brief generated from the wiki, Claude had included only 41 of the 57 facts (72%). When we looked at what had gone wrong, we found that the shared table summarized the contract terms poorly and was missing some of them altogether, because agents had overwritten one another’s work.
The contract terms were the most important part of the wiki, but the instructions didn’t say that. To find better instructions, we ran a loop in which Claude reflected on its failures, proposed a change to the instructions, and rebuilt the wiki. This loop allowed Claude to discover how important it was to follow the contract terms, and it rewrote the instructions for building the wiki accordingly.
Each contract got its own page in the wiki, written by a single agent, which quoted the terms word for word rather than summarizing them into a shared table. When we graded the brief generated from this improved wiki, 100% of the criteria could be met, up from 72%.
Dreaming can fill in gaps a session leaves behind
When Claude writes memory notes, it sees only the context in that particular session. Dreaming can search across many sessions to find common patterns or correct common mistakes.
When building the wikis for the LAB benchmark (as an example, see Figure 14), sometimes Claude would link to pages that hadn’t yet been created. A second dreaming pass closed many of these gaps. On one task (Figure 15), dreaming found nine links to a missing page in the wiki and repointed them to an existing page for the same document. It also added three new pages, including an index, to improve the wiki.
Thinking backward, looking forward
There are many open questions about how to design memory systems for agents. One is when Claude should create memory. When Claude works on a task, it can write to a memory store (e.g., a folder, file, or database), capturing salience at the source and making important details immediately available for future turns or sessions. But logging notes in the moment inevitably draws Claude’s attention away from its main task. In contrast, dreaming can push memory creation to the background, but any outputs from dreaming may not be immediately accessible to Claude.
Another question is how to keep memory reliably up to date. Claude can make incorrect predictions or write rules to memory that are wrong from the start. Memory can also drift and become irrelevant as the environment changes. Dreaming is one way to fix these mistakes (Figure 8), but it requires compute. Directing Claude to continuously check all its memories might not be practical. It may be important instead to focus dreaming on information that’s likely to change or to carefully tune the cadence of dreaming (e.g., based on how often memories are accessed).
It’s also reasonable to consider transcript search as the foundation of Claude’s memory. Combining representations (e.g., wikis) with search (Figure 10) can sometimes be effective. But Claude needs to use compute to maintain memory representations and make sure they don’t accumulate errors or hallucinations.
Even though Claude’s judgment about salience has improved, no model can really know what will happen in future turns. Dreaming gives Claude an opportunity to look backward and use compute to organize its memory, correct mistakes, and maintain representations that allow future sessions to access memory more readily. Memory systems will evolve as Claude gets more capable, and we still have a lot to learn about designing Claude’s memory systems.
See our cookbooks for getting started with dreaming and knowledge wikis.

====================================================================================================

The Components of Agent Memory
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-07
06:39:04 UTC
15:39:04 KST

Søren Kierkegaard wrote that life can only be understood backward, but it must be lived forward. We’ve encountered a similar bind when designing memory for AI agents.
As humans, we encode memory constantly as we go about our lives. The hippocampus plays a central role in this, capturing experiences in the moment so that memories form quickly and are immediately available. But only some experiences are consolidated into long-term knowledge.
Much of that consolidation appears to happen when our brains are offline. As we sleep, and during quiet rest, the hippocampus replays our recent experiences. This is thought to gradually distill those experiences and integrate them into the neocortex—the part of our brains that stores most of what we think of as knowledge.
Agent memory can use a similar division of labor. Agents capture much of their work in logs or transcripts. Researchers at Letta and UC Berkeley proposed sleep-time compute as a way for models to use idle time between interactions to reorganize their existing context. The same idea can help agents organize their memory by reflecting on logs to fix mistakes or produce new notes and representations. This process is often called dreaming (Figure 1).
We’ve found that dreaming has been a useful tool for helping us give Claude effective memory. In this post, we share how we’ve come to think about agent memory and walk through several evaluations we’ve used to explore agent memory, including the use of dreaming, and what each component contributes to memory organization.
Memory notes
By “agent memory,” we mean knowledge that Claude gains from experience—including interactions with users and tasks it’s been assigned—and that it retains between sessions, saved as context it can read later. It excludes other ways models can learn or manage context, such as changes to their weights (the internal parameters set during training), in-context learning (knowledge gained within a single conversation), and compaction (summaries written by Claude that replace its older turns so it can continue working in a new context window).
In the human brain, chemical signals released before, during, or after an experience can mark a memory as important. Marked memories tend to be replayed more often than unmarked ones during offline periods. This process, called replay, helps stabilize and integrate them into long-term memory.
Similarly, Claude can tag salient events in a session log as they happen, so they’re easier to find later, or it can write them to a memory store, such as a folder of memory notes (Figure 2). But how can Claude tell what’s salient in the moment, before it knows what’s coming next?
Memory can be a prediction about what the future will need
Memory is, occasionally, a prediction. By writing down a rule, an agent predicts that the pattern it’s describing will be relevant again. For instance, writing down a verified fact is a prediction that an agent in a future session will want to rely on that fact without double-checking it. Over time, Claude has become much better at making these kinds of predictions.
Consider a task from Continual Learning Bench (CL-Bench) 1.0, a benchmark that measures an agent’s ability to use memory in order to tackle long-horizon tasks. One particular task asks Claude to answer a series of questions by looking up information in a database. After Claude gives an answer, it’s told the correct solution and can write a memory to help answer future questions.
Memory can support a learning loop here. If Claude gets a question wrong, it can record the failure, come up with theories about what went wrong, test those theories, and turn what it finds into a rule about how to answer in the future.
We ran the CL-Bench 1.0 task using Claude Managed Agents. Claude started each question in a fresh session, with one exception: it could retrieve files from a memory directory that persisted from one session to the next.
Different models achieved different levels of success at creating their own learning loops. Claude Sonnet 4.6 got as far as generating theories about why an answer was wrong. Its memory directory contained a scattering of files, each listing several untested guesses.
Claude Opus 4.7 consolidated its notes into a single reference document and began marking which facts it had checked and which were still unconfirmed. Claude Sonnet 5 marked each answer as confirmed after the grader had checked it, and wrote a correction when it had answered wrong.
Claude Opus 5 was the first of these models to close the loop. Most of its entries included a rule (and also how it knew the rule was true), answers to earlier questions, and clear warnings about traps it had discovered along the way (Figure 3).
Like Opus 5, Claude Opus 5.5 and Claude Sonnet 5.5 both closed the loop, using memory to capture validated rules about how to best answer future questions. With memory, both scored about the same as Opus 5, though their baseline performance without memory was slightly higher.
Claude has learned to judge salience
We also evaluated Claude’s ability to play Pokémon Red, a game that challenges Claude to navigate complex long-horizon tasks, as a test of Claude’s judgment about what to save to memory. At the start of each run, we gave each model a folder of memory files that it could read from and write to while it played (Figure 4).
We compared the memory folders from one run each of Claude Sonnet 3.7 and Claude Fable 5, when each was about 10,000 steps into the game. A key difference we noticed was what each model thought was worth remembering. Sonnet 3.7 assumed everything might matter and wrote to memory indiscriminately. Its memory folder contained an archive of encounters, often with a separate file for each battle it fought, each item it found, and each non-player character it met. Ultimately, Sonnet 3.7 wrote 413 memory files across 225 folders and earned one badge (badges mark a player’s progress through the game).
Fable 5, by comparison, wrote nine files in two folders, plus a single top-level file called global.md, and earned four badges.
Sonnet 3.7’s approach was a problem mainly because the game state of Pokémon changes constantly—Sonnet 3.7’s detailed record was continuously going out of date. As a result, as Claude progressed, it had to spend more time and resources combing through its memories rather than playing the game. Around step 10,000, for example, Sonnet 3.7 started its fourth folder of “master” memory files. Over the next 17 steps, it created six files, deleted nine others, and took no actions in the game itself.
Fable 5’s approach used memory for things the game didn’t explicitly tell it. Some of the kinds of information Sonnet 3.7 wrote to memory—such as how much money the player has or where the player is on the map—can be read off the screen every turn, so storing it is redundant. By contrast, memory files about strategy, plans, and tested rules exist only if Claude works them out, and they disappear unless Claude writes them down.
Fable 5 organized its memory accordingly. Its global.md file acted as a dashboard, holding the current goal, a prewritten plan for tackling gyms (the challenges where badges are won), and the model’s own rules for using memory. A locations folder held plans for navigating particular routes, and a gameplay folder held strategies based on what it had learned about how the game works. For example, after seven Poké Balls in a row failed to catch a Magikarp (one of the easiest Pokémon to catch), Claude wrote, “If 3+ balls fail in a row, VARY the timing (insert extra ‘wait’ presses between menu steps) - the catch landed right after doing this.”
With a much smaller memory folder, Fable 5 progressed much further in the game than Sonnet 3.7. Opus 5.5 took a similar approach: it kept a small folder of memory notes that tracked its current goal and next steps, rewrote them as it played, and beat the game.
Steering Claude to write memory
We’ve found that it helps to tell Claude what memory is for, but not how to use it. On CL-Bench, we simply told Claude that a memory folder was available. That general guidance was enough for Opus 4.7 and Opus 5 to use memory effectively, and it gave both a noticeable boost in performance (Figure 3).
We also tested giving the models specific rules about what they should write to memory, with one rule per test. We compared how much each model gained from memory with each rule against how much it gained without it. When we set a rule to “record only failures,” both Opus 5 and Opus 4.7 lost much of the performance boost that memory gave them (Figure 5, right). For stronger models, giving them freedom to judge salience was more effective than setting fixed rules in advance, and those rules could end up weakening performance.
We also noticed that steering helped older models the most. For instance, Sonnet 4.6 often didn’t read files from its memory directory and failed to test its theories about why an answer was wrong.
In this case, prompting Claude to read its memory files before answering—and then to explicitly test its theories—nearly doubled its benchmark score (Figure 5, left). Sonnet 5 does this on its own. Without being prompted to, it read its memory before answering 9 out of 10 times. By comparison, Sonnet 4.6 did this less than half of the time.
With frontier Claude models, we typically give simple guidance to create memory notes when a person asks them to or when something is clearly worth remembering, and we leave that judgment up to Claude.
Dreaming can correct memory
Since Claude writes to memory based on what it judges to be salient in-session, a note that’s wrong from the start or that drifts as the environment changes can be read, and trusted, by Claude in a later session.
Dreaming gives Claude a way to test its notes against the original transcripts (Figure 6). We saw a positive example of this when Claude played Pokémon using Managed Agents with a mounted directory for its memories.
In one part of the game, the player has to get through Mt. Moon, a cave system with its entrance on the west side of the map and its exit on the east. Near the entrance is a trap: a one-way ledge that drops the player onto the route they came from, with no clear way to climb back up. Claude was using the PokéCenter, a building at the mountain’s entrance, as a landmark, so whether it walked off the ledge depended on which side of the mountain it thought the PokéCenter was on.
At one point, Claude wrote down (incorrectly) that the PokéCenter was on the east side. When it walked out of the PokéCenter, it announced, “Mt Moon is behind me,” and updated its notes: “Mt Moon DONE.” But Mt. Moon was not behind it. A few actions later, Claude went over the ledge and landed back on the route it had come from (Figure 7). Its navigation tool didn’t show the drop, so Claude concluded, “I overshot far west,” and that it had been on the correct side all along. It hadn’t.
We then tested whether dreaming could help Claude avoid this trap. We ran dreaming on the memory folder that contained the wrong location for the PokéCenter: Claude checked the note against the session transcript, caught its error, and rewrote it. The transcript gave Claude a ground truth to validate its memory against. With the corrected memory note, Claude went straight back into Mt. Moon and eventually reached Cerulean City.
In the run with the uncorrected note, Claude dropped back onto that earlier route three times. After the third failure, Claude seemed to give up, writing, “I’m going to stop rather than keep looping.” Our setup’s automatic reply instructed it to continue. So it did, all the way back to Pewter City, the town before the mountain, where it hoped to heal and restock. The run ended without Claude ever reaching Cerulean City.
Dreaming can build new memory representations
With dreaming, Claude can build representations to help navigate memory (Figure 9). One type of memory representation it uses is called a wiki, a collection of pages written and maintained by Claude that gathers what Claude knows about one subject and links it to other notes. These links effectively provide Claude with a map it can use to sift through its memory and efficiently locate memories when they’re needed.
Representations like wikis can help Claude traverse its ever-growing knowledge base. We explored the benefit of wikis as a memory representation using a corpus of internal documents—about 350,000 files, roughly 420M tokens—from Stainless, a software company we acquired in May. We created an evaluation set of 25 questions about this corpus and gave Claude a search tool to explore it. We also instructed Claude to build a wiki based on these files using dreaming. Over 128 sessions, we prompted Opus 5 agents to explore the files, write or correct linked wiki pages, merge duplicates, and check claims against the sources.
Each session built on the progress that agents made in prior sessions, using a shared log to coordinate work. As we scaled dreaming from two sessions to 128, performance on the 25 questions grew from 13% to 48% of graded criteria (Figure 10).
Claude builds its wikis incrementally. After creating an initial wiki, it uses subsequent rounds of dreaming to add to, edit, or prune it. In the early rounds of dreaming, Claude’s knowledge is like that of a new employee, and its ability to answer questions based on the wiki alone is limited. The more it dreams, the richer its knowledge map grows, like that of a seasoned employee. In our tests, we found that the wiki helped Claude the most when we also gave it access to a search tool. When it used both search and the wiki, Claude’s aggregate score rose from 54% to 61% as that wiki grew, compared with 53% when it had access to the search tool alone. Claude’s best score was 48% when it used only the wiki to answer questions. Using search and the wiki also saved about 20,000 tokens per question.
We wanted to test which kinds of tasks benefited from Claude having a wiki. To do this, we used the Harvey LAB benchmark along with a collection of documents about a merger. The benchmark gives Claude a set of documents and asks Claude to generate a legal brief based on their content. The benchmark then grades the brief against a list of criteria. While a tad less engaging, perhaps, than a Pokémon run, these evaluations give us diverse collections of documents to build wikis from.
We instructed Claude to build a wiki from the documents. We then asked Claude to answer questions (about the merger) or generate legal briefs (the LAB benchmark) both from the wiki and from a direct file search. One test asked 24 questions about the merger (Figure 11, left), more than half of which needed Claude to find information across several documents, and many of which also needed Claude to use judgment or do math. On those items (red and orange lines), Claude scored 87% when it consulted the wiki, compared with 73% when it used search alone.
In other cases, the wiki was less useful. On one LAB task (Figure 11, right), brief criteria often could be retrieved with a text search from a single document, and the wiki showed no clear gain in performance relative to search. On another LAB task, the wiki grew larger than the documents themselves (Figure 12). Like the mapmakers in Borges’s one-paragraph short story “On Exactitude in Science,” who drafted a map so detailed it was as large as the empire it represented, Claude had made an extremely detailed wiki that effectively abstracted nothing.
Dreaming can promote notes up the memory hierarchy
Dreaming can also float notes to the top of the memory hierarchy, and the agent harness can preemptively load them into Claude’s context at the start of every session (Figure 13). This means Claude doesn’t need to search for them, or even to know there’s something to search for.
Promoting memories into a new session (a form of context injection) must be done carefully. Injected memories use up valuable tokens, and an incorrect note can be carried into every session. Loading a note also doesn’t guarantee that Claude acts on it: our documentation on Claude Code warns that longer instruction files reduce adherence. So the top layer of the memory hierarchy has to stay relatively small, and much of dreaming’s work here is keeping it that way—by moving some details down the hierarchy and leaving links behind.
Claude Code’s auto memory keeps its injected layer small in the same way, with Claude maintaining it during the session. Claude doesn’t load all of its auto memory into every session’s context window. It loads only the first 200 lines or 25 KB of MEMORY.md. This header serves as an index, which shows Claude what it knows and where to find it.
Dreaming can focus memory on a specific goal
Dreaming can also shape how memory grows. An assistant like Claude Duet, for example, often benefits from having memories that are organized around the person it’s helping, and dreaming can be tuned to build memory in service of a particular goal.
The instructions that guide how memory grows differ by task and tell Claude what information actually matters. For example, one task in the LAB benchmark simulates a company acquisition and provides a set of contracts. The brief is graded on whether it correctly describes the contract terms.
We started with basic instructions for generating the wiki that didn’t tell Claude how to use the wiki. This task relied heavily on contract terms. While the wiki was being built, several agents wrote to a shared table of contract terms at the same time (Figure 14, left).
The result was a wiki that was missing key facts. When we graded a brief generated from the wiki, Claude had included only 41 of the 57 facts (72%). When we looked at what had gone wrong, we found that the shared table summarized the contract terms poorly and was missing some of them altogether, because agents had overwritten one another’s work.
The contract terms were the most important part of the wiki, but the instructions didn’t say that. To find better instructions, we ran a loop in which Claude reflected on its failures, proposed a change to the instructions, and rebuilt the wiki. This loop allowed Claude to discover how important it was to follow the contract terms, and it rewrote the instructions for building the wiki accordingly.
Each contract got its own page in the wiki, written by a single agent, which quoted the terms word for word rather than summarizing them into a shared table. When we graded the brief generated from this improved wiki, 100% of the criteria could be met, up from 72%.
Dreaming can fill in gaps a session leaves behind
When Claude writes memory notes, it sees only the context in that particular session. Dreaming can search across many sessions to find common patterns or correct common mistakes.
When building the wikis for the LAB benchmark (as an example, see Figure 14), sometimes Claude would link to pages that hadn’t yet been created. A second dreaming pass closed many of these gaps. On one task (Figure 15), dreaming found nine links to a missing page in the wiki and repointed them to an existing page for the same document. It also added three new pages, including an index, to improve the wiki.
Thinking backward, looking forward
There are many open questions about how to design memory systems for agents. One is when Claude should create memory. When Claude works on a task, it can write to a memory store (e.g., a folder, file, or database), capturing salience at the source and making important details immediately available for future turns or sessions. But logging notes in the moment inevitably draws Claude’s attention away from its main task. In contrast, dreaming can push memory creation to the background, but any outputs from dreaming may not be immediately accessible to Claude.
Another question is how to keep memory reliably up to date. Claude can make incorrect predictions or write rules to memory that are wrong from the start. Memory can also drift and become irrelevant as the environment changes. Dreaming is one way to fix these mistakes (Figure 8), but it requires compute. Directing Claude to continuously check all its memories might not be practical. It may be important instead to focus dreaming on information that’s likely to change or to carefully tune the cadence of dreaming (e.g., based on how often memories are accessed).
It’s also reasonable to consider transcript search as the foundation of Claude’s memory. Combining representations (e.g., wikis) with search (Figure 10) can sometimes be effective. But Claude needs to use compute to maintain memory representations and make sure they don’t accumulate errors or hallucinations.
Even though Claude’s judgment about salience has improved, no model can really know what will happen in future turns. Dreaming gives Claude an opportunity to look backward and use compute to organize its memory, correct mistakes, and maintain representations that allow future sessions to access memory more readily. Memory systems will evolve as Claude gets more capable, and we still have a lot to learn about designing Claude’s memory systems.
See our cookbooks for getting started with dreaming and knowledge wikis.

AI broke the cybersecurity world. What’s next?
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-07
05:43:11 UTC
14:43:11 KST

Today, I’m talking with Alex Stamos, chief security officer at Cognition and longtime cybersecurity expert, about what a mess cybersecurity is right now.
AI broke the cybersecurity world. What’s next?
Going inside the security ‘dark ages’ with Alex Stamos.
This episode is part one of a special series looking at AI in real life.
I’ve always enjoyed talking to Alex because he’s so direct, and you’re going to hear him say that the multibillion-dollar cybersecurity industry as we’ve known it is essentially overdue to the unrelenting, AI-boosted waves of hacks, breaches, and attacks — and this is only the beginning of how much AI is changing security.
Verge subscribers, don’t forget you get exclusive access to ad-free Decoder wherever you get your podcasts. Head here. Not a subscriber? You can sign up here.
Both private actors and also state actors, like Russia or North Korea, are breaking into systems all over thanks to AI helping them both detect and exploit vulnerabilities all at the same time. It’s got a real frontier feel to it, Alex notes, just like the ’90s, when the whole idea of an always-online world was brand-new.
That much disruption means a lot of places where bad guys can get in while everyone else is left scrambling. Alex isn’t a doomer in the long run, but he does think we’re in for a “dark age” while we try to figure out how on earth to adapt to the new era.
There’s a lot going on here — we even got all the way to how AI-powered misinformation and slop might be managed on our social networks.
Before we get going, do me a favor and subscribe to Decoder on YouTube, where we put out new episodes every Monday and Thursday.
Okay: Alex Stamos on how AI is upending cybersecurity. Here we go.
This interview has been lightly edited for length and clarity.
Alex Stamos, you’re the chief security officer at Cognition. You’re also a lecturer at Stanford University Cyber Policy Center. Welcome back to Decoder.
Hey, thanks for having me, man.
I’m excited to talk to you. It’s been a long time since you and I chatted in what you might call the “content moderation era” of internet policy discourse. I feel like you and I talked a lot about how all that was working, about election security, all those sorts of things. We’re in a new era of big problems on the internet, particularly around cyber defense, cybersecurity. You’ve been thinking about this stuff a lot. You referred to the past few weeks as “security awareness month.” Tell me what’s going on. Just give me the lay of land right now.
October is traditionally security awareness month. Lots of companies run events and deal both internally and then tell people, “Change your password, use multiple passwords, use a password manager.” What has happened over the last month is we’ve had a couple of these big bugs, PHP, Node and such. What’s been less discussed is a huge number of companies have been breached.
You have a series of AI-related companies, the neoclouds that have been breached in succession. You have the Binance hack, which is the largest heist of anything in human history. You have the Cursor incident where one of the things that’s going on here is these hacks are being chained together. From what we know right now, one of the companies that was broken into was Fireworks, which is a prominent provider of AI services to a number of companies. From the Fireworks hack, API keys were stolen that allowed people to break into Cursor. Once they got into Cursor, they then pushed a bad plugin, which went to 5,000 more companies and that caused 5,000 potential breaches. It’s just this kind of permanent continuous breach that is going on throughout the tech industry.
A CISO friend of mine said to a bunch of us, “Is anybody else tired?” And everybody said yes, because effectively we have been at DEFCON 2 for all of October and now going into November.
I’m going to ask you some really dumb questions here. Who is behind all these attacks? Is it Russia? Is it North Korea? Do we know?
It’s a variety of different groups. It is not just because adversaries have decided, but what has happened is, from my perspective, we are at the start of the takeoff of adversary capability enabled by AI, that the open-weight models have now gotten to the point at which you can automate most of the kill chain. This “kill chain” term is something we stole from the military. In the military, they mean to actually kill something. They’re the steps you do to blow something up. In cyber, the kill chain is the things you do to have an effect on a target. So to steal information, to turn something off, to steal money, to steal Bitcoin.
Traditionally, you do research upfront, you look at a piece of software, you find a vulnerability, you write exploits, and you do that all way ahead of time. And then you have a human being go and take that stuff you built and deploy it against either one target — because maybe you’re a government and you’re going against Lockheed Martin or you’re going against another government or you’re a ransomware actor — and you take all that work you did and you deploy it against hundreds or thousands of targets really fast. Now with AI, what’s happening is this kill chain has inverted where you can run an AI system that just goes hog wild and it looks at the targets and it finds the bugs in real time and it writes the exploits in real time and it breaks into these companies. One of the things that’s happening is that these systems are going and they’re so good at finding bugs that they are finding the bugs in real time and writing the exploits in real time.
The system that everybody’s talking about is not the only one, but the system we’re talking about is a thing called ARTEX, which is a Chinese open-source tool that anybody can go look at. There’s an English translation on GitHub that you can check out. And if you power it on the back end by something like an obliterated GLM-5.3, it will let you break into a shocking number of organizations. This started with three South Korean banks. This week, about 200 more companies have been hit. I’ve been helping a couple of companies. Cognition is not an incident response company, but we do go in and help people fix their bugs afterwards by using our tools. It is shocking the companies that are getting hit by something you could just download. It has been a long time since you’ve been able to go and just download an open-source tool and break into multi-billion dollar companies. It’s like the ‘90s. That’s-
That’s what I was thinking about. I think about Windows in the late ‘90s, early 2000s when it was a security disaster.
I started saying this actually in the beginning of this year, that it’s an incredibly exciting and terrifying time, because the last time it felt like this was when I was a much younger man and I would go to the very early DEFCONs. DEFCON these days is like 40,000–60,000 people. Back then [in the 1990s] it was a couple hundred people and it was one track and you’d go in and somebody would give a talk and you’d leave the room and you’d think, “Every company in the world is vulnerable to this attack.” The difference was that the entire world didn’t run on the internet. You could go do bad things, but you couldn’t ruin the world and you certainly couldn’t automate it in the way you can now.
It’s back to those days where tools you could just get and build yourself could do crazy things. It’s back to the Wild West days. Back in those days, there were a couple hundred people who had those skills and we were all in one room in Las Vegas and most of them were actually good guys. They didn’t want to be criminals. And now you have a much larger ecosystem of people, many of whom live in places where the law enforcement can’t touch and who have a big economic incentive and have access to these tools. The other big thing is they no longer have to use frontier models that have protections, that have logs that are available to law enforcement. My M5 Mac Ultra Studio finally showed up and I’ve been running a quantized obliterated GLM-5.3 and oh my God, what you can do is incredible.
You mentioned the economics of frontier models. The economy of cyber is one of those things that has gotten sophisticated over the years. One of the things that our systems got safer is you could pay the number of people at DEF CON a whole bunch of money to protect all the systems and it was economically smart to pay them to be defenders instead of attackers. I’m just rewinding my own brain through this history. There was a long conversation in our industry about security through obscurity by hiding all the bugs and hiding all the code versus open source and being really transparent. There was an etiquette that developed about how to reveal the bugs and when to give people notification. There were transparency reports and bug bounties.
You could call it an etiquette, you could call it an economy. We built a culture around cybersecurity and cyber defense and how the attackers and defenders would operate. What you are describing changes it all for a whole number of reasons, but the one that sticks out to me is the economics of it. I can go buy a Mac Studio, I can get an open-weight model and I can just start and that does not place me inside of the culture. There’s not going to be some old man, an Obi-Wan Kenobi saying, “Here’s how you act like a cybersecurity Jedi.” I’m just going to start doing stuff. Tell me how that’s changing right now.
Effectively, the last era of security from my perspective started with the Microsoft, the Bill Gates “trustworthy computing” memo in the early 2000s. And that is when we started designing how we do software security and these big companies, Microsoft and Oracle and then later Amazon and Salesforce and these folks, started hiring ex-hackers. I was part of that generation of the ex-hackers who were at DEF CON and Black Hat and got hired by the companies to go break their stuff. We started coming up with responsible disclosure, bug bounties, consulting companies, so the Foundstone and @stake and then Mandiant and CrowdStrike, these companies grow up and we get this huge industry that is now RSA and Black Hat. This multi-billion dollar security industry grew up in the 2000s and 2010s.
That era is over, like you said, because it was based upon a couple of assumptions. One was that there was a hierarchy of skillsets of people who could do this stuff. You had the script kiddies who could download tools to do things and then the next level up of the people who could write those tools and that was a more rarefied skill set. There were the vulnerability researchers and then there were the gods, and that god tier was in the hundreds of people and those are the people who could do the really, really good vulnerability research, who could write the really, really good shell code, who could reverse engineer patches from Microsoft or somebody like that.
There was also the assumption that there were a limited number of people who could be operational in an attack. If you wanted to build an attack team, then you have a small R&D component and then you have a much larger operational component. If you were a professional attack team at U.S. Cyber Command, at Russia SVR, at Chinese MSS, that you build the structure of your R&D team of elite people and then your less elite operational people, and that was copied then by the Russian ransomware actors. You have to have a conspiracy of people to do this stuff. A lot of this stuff also fell apart with the invention of Bitcoin because one of the assumptions was that you had to have people who could launder money and Bitcoin meant that nerds could launder money. So that started to change the economics of the entire thing.
All this stuff is falling apart because of AI, because the skillset issue is that now script kiddies have a bunch of the skillset of the Dave Vitales and the other cyber gods who could do these things, such that with this Mac that’s sitting on my desktop, I can reverse engineer a Microsoft patch and walk through and then write stable exploit code. It’s not quite there yet to be automatic. You still have to have a bunch of skills, but we’re really, really close.
What’s really concerning, and what you see with the ARTEX stuff, is you can now kind of let loose on the operational side. We were never secure. It was all a fantasy. These vulnerabilities have always existed. These companies have never been safe. And that is what we’ve seen over the last decade is these mostly Russian ransomware actors nailing the mid-size companies that most people have never heard of that are actually really important, like some midsize industrial company that makes some widget that we all depend upon. I once did work for the largest distributor of medical and dental supplies in the country, and they got really hit badly by a Russian ransomware actor and then dental offices across the country were canceling appointments. Didn’t even make the news, but it was because they couldn’t get gloves, because they couldn’t get supplies. It’s because a company like that’s got one security guy and a 10 person, 12 person IT team and they’re going up against a 20 person Russian ransomware team, they’re toast.
But it’s just because there’s only a handful of Russian teams that could do that. Now two or three Russian kids can have a whole bank of Macs or, if they have the money, they can buy Nvidia H-100s and they can run a ton of agents who do this all day and that is what is changing.
I’m sorry, you asked who was doing this. For Binance, it’s North Koreans and they have an entire team where all they do is steal Bitcoin. Other people have gotten out of it because it’s really hard to compete against a foreign intelligence agency that recruits their smartest kids and all they do is steal Bitcoin. They’re extremely good at it. And for the Binance hack, we could talk about it. There’s probably some AI component, but mostly that was actually human intelligence and very, very smart social engineering and the North Koreans are... I mean, you have to give it to them. They’re very, very good at that.
But for the other stuff, it looks like for the people who are breaking into the AI companies is what’s called the Chinese cottage access industry, which are small Chinese contractors who break into companies and then sell the information to much larger Chinese companies.
That diffusion of skill changing the economics of cyber is really fascinating to me. We ran a headline ages ago called “Attack of the Killer Script Kiddies” at the very beginning of all this, and I feel bad because it is much more suited to now than it was to then, because then it was just a lot of automated mischief. Now —
Because you wasted the headline. You wish you held it back.
We wasted the headline. Every now and again you get a lightning bolt of a headline. You got to get it out there. It’s a great piece of art. We’ll put it in the show notes, but it feels like the diffusion you’re talking about is not that a bunch of teenagers are going to be even more malicious. It’s a bunch of bad actors with real incentives to do harm are now vastly more capable than they were before. And that doesn’t seem like you can re-jigger the economics of that unless you somehow make it really expensive to do the attacks.
I think it’s going to be extremely hard to make it expensive to do the attacks.
We’re going to have to lower the cost of defense. You’re completely right to look at this economic problem because what’s happening is attacks are becoming agentic. The cost per token for attackers is going towards zero, and the cost of defense for defenders is staying quite high. It is very important for us to find ways for defenders to have cheaper options. I think that is critical. That’s something I am personally extremely focused on. To be frank, very few people are.
There are a couple of problems here. One, there’s a ton of focus on the defensive side of AI on finding and fixing bugs. That’s great. It’s important. But software security as a CISO… I work for an AI software company, Cognition. Even as a CISO of a software product company, software security is 25% of my job. I have all kinds of other responsibilities. And software security is the part of my job that has been both the most revolutionized and the most improved by AI. The other 75% has been helped a bit and in some cases made much worse. There’s so much more we need to do than just hill climb on finding and fixing bugs. There’s been so much less hill climbing on all these other things. And the next area needs to be the operational security side.
Wait, give me an example of the other things, the 75%.
Okay. So the operational security side is watching your logs, saying, “Ah, somebody is running our techs against us. I am now going to trigger an investigation. I am going to look to see what is going on and I’m going to make an intelligent decision of what I am going to do to respond to this without shutting down the entire company.” That is SOC (security operations center) work. Companies that want to survive this era are going to have to replace human beings who do that work with the first tier and then eventually the second tier with AI. And you can do that with current LLMs.
The first tier actually has to be not an LLM. It has to be something much dumber because you’re looking at terabytes or petabytes of logs and it is just completely uneconomical to do that with an actual LLM. We have other things — TF-IDFs, MLBERTs, traditional classifiers — that can operate much faster, but then that sets off an alarm and then you send it to a small LLM and then the small LLM calls its bigger brother and that might call an even bigger brother.
But for those models, we need to do a lot more work to optimize them and we need to make them cheap. I have a colleague who works at one of the large security platform companies who said that doing an investigation on one of the big frontier model cyber models costs $900 on their product to do an investigation. That is not going to work when the Russian ransomware guys are either using Macs or they’re using throwaway crypto mining rigs and their marginal cost is zero.
This is one of the things we’re working on at Cognition: We train our own models, retrain open weights, and we’re trying to drive this cost way down. It’s something we’ve done, but we have a lot more work to do here and a lot of other people have work to do here. This is where, as an industry, we have to focus on the Pareto frontier between cost and efficiency on cyber because it’s not just bug-finding.
The other place here is, people talk about Mythos. Mythos is great, Astra Cyber is great. They’re incredible models, but even the people who have access to it, which is a tiny number of companies, they only scan their most important code. And there are hundreds of billions of lines of code out there that are not the Linux kernel, that are the crap C# and J2E code that was written by Infosys 15 years ago. All of that code is not going to go through a frontier cyber model that costs a gazillion dollars. That’s the kind of stuff that we need to work on cost efficiency for.
It feels like you’re describing this cliche that I hear all the time: Everything’s going to get so much worse before it gets totally secure forever. And it doesn’t seem like the model you’re describing leads to that result. We’re not going to fix all the bugs forever in some valley of despair and then always have perfect code. At some point, there’s some cost advantage to the attacker, always. You’re running a company, you can’t write defense that has zero marginal costs. Someone has to do it even if the attackers are so infinite that their marginal cost is effectively zero. So are we going to go through a period where everything is so much worse and then it’s perfect forever? Or how does the cost manage itself out, into balance?
I am neither the hardest doomer nor the most positive person about this. I feel like we are not going to be doomed. We’re not all going to die or be uploaded to the singularity in the next two years, nor do I think when things get better, they’re going to be perfect. The metaphor I’ve been using is that we are entering a dark age. Things are going to get more bleak and the responsibility of those of us who work in cybersecurity — especially those of us who work in cybersecurity at the AI companies, and we are a lab if a smaller one compared to the big-L Labs — is to help shorten that dark age.
The Dark Ages were bad, but we survived them. And then we had the Enlightenment and the Renaissance, but it’s not like the Renaissance was perfect. It wasn’t utopia and everything was perfect. We will still have hacks. All kinds of bad things happen even when you don’t have a security flaw. There are tons of security breaches that do not require a flaw in the software itself. And we still will have flaws in software, even if LLMs are writing all of them in strong languages and it will take a long period of time to get rid of all the flaws. I do think, however, things will start to get better as the median code becomes code that was written by an LLM in a memory-safe and type-safe language. But the problem is we have a couple of decades of stuff that human beings wrote and that wrote in languages that we should have replaced years ago.
My feeling is that to shorten this age, we have to do a couple of things. One is we have to stop to get out of this like we’re just going to find bugs and patch them. A bunch of stuff just needs to be refactored. If you’re looking at your code base and you scan it and you’ve got 10,000 bugs, then probably now we are at the point where rewriting it is actually cheaper. That is my observation from looking at these customers of ours: If you can get a working build, then it is actually much easier to write a whole humongous suite of tests and to spec out how this thing works and then to rebuild it within that spec and those tests securely than it is to find all the bugs and fix them. It actually is.
I don’t know what is going on through the mind of the Linux kernel developers, but again, in that talk I gave at the beginning of this year, I posited two possible futures and one was that we’re now at superhuman bug finding. Maybe we just have a pool of superhuman bugs and we’re going to drain it. That’s the good future.
But the bad future is every time we get a new model, there’s a new superhuman bug pool that’s a little more superhuman. And unfortunately we seem to be closer to that in that the Linux kernel got a bunch of fixes for Mythos and then Mythos Preview and then we had Mythos 5.1 and there was another pool. And then we got Astra Cyber and we just got another 1,000 Linux kernel patches. It’s not slowing down yet. I don’t think we’re just going to scan our way through the C code of the Linux kernel. There are only two companies, honestly, in the world who could probably do this. It’d be Google or Amazon.
I wish instead of the Fuchsia project or rebooting the Google book or the Chromebook again, Google would take all of those incredibly brilliant engineers and just tell them, “Take the Linux kernel and rewrite this in Rust.” That would be their gift to the world. And it would be an incredible advertisement for Gemini, for Carbon or whatever would be like, “Get rid of all the drivers that nobody needs. This thing doesn’t have to run on the 46 DX3 or whatever.” I mean, I know it doesn’t, but you know what I’m saying.

And then just release it and call it whatever you want, the G Linux kernel, just like they did this with OpenSSL. And I would run that. But for whatever reason, the technology industry has not been able to get together and make these hard decisions of these fundamental things that underlie the entire human race, we have to completely rebuild.

I’m a little over my skis here because there are people much smarter than me that study this.

There’s a lot going on in this conversation already. My pessimism about all these things is very simply expressed. Americans in particular, we are very bad at doing anything until a bad thing happens. We love a cure. We hate prevention. A lot of bad things have happened. There’s the Hugging Face attack. The Hugging Face incident was good, and it did wake up a lot of people in security. We are a little bit better off because Hugging Face happened and we saw what was going to be possible a couple of months later in open source. But you’re totally right. We as Americans — and Americans are the only ones honestly who can lead the world on this, we’re the ones building the AI for the most part. We’re the ones who lead the cybersecurity industry. The Chinese are mostly enabling the attackers, not the defenders. They should enable defense. They want to be participants in the global economy. Something I have learned now, working at Cognition, at an AI startup, is that you can just do things. Seriously. You can just go do things. We just need to have more agency, as a tech industry. We should just go do things. That should be the lesson of the AI era. We should not be so risk-averse in doing the right thing, security-wise. If I had a message for other CISOs, is just go do the right thing. Because CISOs are, by our nature, risk-averse. We are the people who manage downside risk. We have all L, and no P, in the P&L. And I have had to personally break through that natural inclination, because you can’t just play defense.
But this is my prevention versus cure. The prevention is cheap, but it’s all loss, on the P&L statement. It’s all just cost. If the bad thing happens, you say, “We have to do anything to fix this, because there’s an economic benefit to having a functional business again.” And it seems like in this moment, even a basic understanding of that risk versus cost is totally scrambled, and no one has quite figured it out yet.
Yeah, I totally agree. I’ll give you a very practical example of a way CISOs and their bosses are going to have to change on this. Every company in the next… I was going to say six months, but really in the next 90 days, is going to have to RTX itself.
You’re going to have to run the attack on yourself.
Yes. If not with real RTX, you’re going to have to use an AI red teaming product that is functionally equivalent, or you’re going to have to hire a company to do that for yourself. If you do not, you will get breached.
That’s terrifying, right? Because these things are completely non-deterministic, by definition. They are very smart, they are very fast. They are so fast that no matter what, if you hire an AI pentest, an AI red team company, they will promise you verbally, “It is completely safe.” And then they will give you a contract that says, “Our product will give you cancer, will immanentize the eschaton, and will likely end the human race. And you hold us harmless when any of these things happen.” There is a disconnect between what they will say verbally to you, and what their lawyers have written. Because that company has by definition only existed for six months, or at least the product has existed for six months, if it’s an older company. They have no idea what their product is going to do when it meets your software. They have no idea, whatever they say to you.
But you have to do it, because some dude is going to do it to you, and you need to know what is going to happen and fix it. Maybe those people will break your system, they might cause downtime, but at least what they’re not going to do is steal your data and ransom it, or intentionally shut down your blood bank refrigerators. That is what happened in France. But that is exactly the kind of thing that a conservative CISO would never even ask to do, and a conservative company, even if they have a risk-taking CISO, would never let their CISO do.
What’s going to happen in the next six months? A ton of companies are going to get breached by some 19-year-old running RTX, and then buying tokens off OpenRouter, or running it locally on their gaming PC. They’re going to turn off Call of Duty, run RTX, then turn Call of Duty back on. While they did that, they broke into a bank who, their policy said, “can never red team ourselves.” Or they said yes, but they ended up in a 30-day negotiation because their lawyers are like, “You have to have $100 million in insurance, and we want to have this kind of…” They say yes, but their vendor risk management process and their legal process is so onerous, there’s no possible way they could actually accept the risk.
That is my message also. You are going to have to take that risk to actively red team yourself, to front run the bad guys who are doing that right now. Right now, as we speak. And as people are listening to this 24 hours from now, you possibly have an AI system who is trying every single API endpoint, and website that you might not know you have, because it was like a marketing site that was set up a year ago for an executive event. And is going to find a bug, and get in, and then is going to move incredibly fast through your network. So you’d rather pay somebody, or have your own team do that. You need to accept that risk. Almost no company larger than a couple of hundred people has a risk management framework that will allow them to do that.
There are several costs associated with this. And one I think is just the most common in business, which is, “If we find out there’s a problem, we’ll have to pay to fix it. So what if we just wait?” You can see that coming. “What if we find out that we are catastrophically in trouble?” And then there’s, “How much will it cost to fix it?” And then there’s, “What will we do to fix it?” The argument that I’ve heard about “what will we do to fix it” is, “We’ll just unleash a swarm of defensive AI tools that are watching the networks and watching the code, and they’ll make a bunch of autonomous decisions and they’ll fight the autonomous script kiddies who are armed with RTX on their Mac Studios.”
I’ve heard, for example, Palo Alto Network’s CEO Nikesh Arora has made this argument. “You need to give the defensive agents some autonomous capabilities.” And I say, “Hold on. OpenAI can’t control the swarm that went and attacked Hugging Face. How would I control the defensive agent from doing something equally damaging to prevent an attack?” I don’t think that there’s a technical solution there that makes it clear what to do once you’ve found all of the vulnerabilities.

The actual “swarm” from OpenAI was something between, at different times, 700–1,400 agents. Almost nobody is going to be attacked by a swarm. If somebody says “swarm,” they do not actually understand, at the bottom technical level, what’s going on. The RTX attacks are almost certainly one single agent, maybe two.

A couple of things are correct. For one, we are going to have to turn over defensive actions, at least the first couple of levels, to AI. That has already been true, just because any reasonably sized organization generates terabytes of logs, if not petabytes of logs. So you already have AI, if not LLMs then much more traditional AI classifiers looking at it, but then actual decisions need to be turned over to AI. With human oversight. That is true.
There is no way to just release defensive agents on your network, but you can build defensive agents that are plugged into lots of things. They’re plugged into your logs, they’re plugged into your EDR, they’re plugged into your cloud, and they have the instructions on what they’re allowed to do. My preference is there are deterministic limits to what they’re allowed to do. Those are hard to necessarily create ahead of time, but they are important. So for example, we are building all this on top of Devon, our own SOC, but we are putting in place deterministic limits. It has the ability to turn off an account in Okta, but only so many accounts, so it can’t turn off the entire company.
If it goes nuts and it says, “Everybody’s a North Korean,” it can’t turn off everybody in the company. If it hits its limit, it pages us a lot and says, “I hit my limit.” Then a human being has to then approve anybody above that limit. Obviously, if it turns off five people, then something has gone really, really wrong. So, yes, you need defense, but there’s no way to just let a bunch of bots loose to do all the defense.
The only way we’re going to make it through this is that you have to autonomously fix software bugs. I am currently helping a company where we are onboarding all of their repositories. We are scanning with Mythos and a bunch of other models, and we are finding and fixing thousands of bugs. Verifying with tests that we are writing, merging those PRs automatically. If you are renovating old code, the only way you’re going to survive this era is to do that on an automatic basis. That would take years, with humans, and you can do it in days. Certainly that is the place I would start. The next step is the operational side, for sure.

Certainly, there are a bunch of people in these companies, as you know, who want to slow down. A lot of that discussion is driven by the P(doom)ers who [are worried] about AI killing us. You and I are both, I think, old enough to remember, I grew up a couple miles from a SAC base and we did drills where I was taught that hiding beneath my public school desk would save me from a Soviet thermonuclear bomb.
